3 Test-Time Compute

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

When is another token, candidate, check, or model call worth its cost?

An agent that deliberates before acting pays latency and money for a better chance of success, whether it spends them on a longer derivation, more candidate patches, an extra check, or another model call. Each purchase answers a different question. More tokens re-examine what the prompt already holds, more candidates raise the chance that a good one sits somewhere in the pool, and only an executed check adds a fact the model did not have. None of them makes the returned answer correct, because a selector can return a plausible failure, and a search that optimizes against a flawed check finds the flaw faster than the fix. Deliberation is therefore a budgeted allocation problem with a stopping rule. The runtime decides where the next unit of work goes, how far a passing check can be trusted, and when to stop, abstain, or escalate instead of returning its best unverified guess. Every extra turn spent inside one decision lengthens the horizon, so that spending has to be bounded before the agent acts on anything.

Learning Objectives
  • Compare depth, breadth, and feedback by the information each adds, the tokens it consumes, and its critical path.
  • Distinguish candidate availability from selected-output quality when evaluating voting, learned scoring, and task-specific checks.
  • Calculate accepted-set precision from stated checker error rates and explain how adaptive search can change those assumptions.
  • Represent a candidate plan as a dependency graph and identify which parts require revision after an observation changes.
  • Design stopping rules that enforce resource ceilings without treating budget exhaustion as acceptance evidence.
  • Evaluate competing deliberation policies under a common budget and deadline by pass probability, actual spend, and abstentions.

3.1 Limits of a Single Response

The repair agent (lighthouse 1.1) inspects the connection-pool implementation and receives a completed candidate patch. The invocation contract admits it as a candidate, yet the patch rests on an incomplete diagnosis. Client requests intermittently stall even after idle connections become available. Two distinct failure modes can produce this symptom: an aggressive timeout configuration that aborts prematurely, or a lost notification when a worker thread returns a connection to the pool. When prompted, the language model proposes extending the timeout. That change offers a plausible workaround, but it provides no evidence that the timeout caused the stalls. Deploying such a patch immediately risks masking the latency tail while leaving the concurrency defect intact.

Deliberation extends the horizon with extra model turns and checks inside one decision, and hard ceilings bound that spend.

Before committing to an edit, the agent runtime can invest computational work in several distinct ways. It can grant the model a longer derivation budget to trace the lock acquisition and release sequence in detail; it can sample diverse candidate patches that modify different components of the pooling logic; or it can execute a targeted harness to record whether a waiting thread actually receives the condition variable notification. Each strategy purchases a different kind of evidence. Extended internal reasoning can expose subtle logical flaws in the source text. Sampling across temperature or prompt variations can surface novel implementations that a greedy single pass misses. An instrumented test, by contrast, delivers empirical observations of runtime state, which reveal dynamic interactions that static token generation alone cannot establish.

This division marks the operational boundary between internal computation and empirical observation. Additional model computation re-evaluates information already present in the prompt context. Within a single generation, a model can backtrack and revise an earlier analytical misstep even as the previous tokens remain frozen in its context prefix. However, pure computation cannot generate new empirical facts. Only an executed test reports how the running process behaves, and its conclusions remain strictly bounded by the specific conditions and interleavings that the test harness exercised.

Suppose the targeted test forces an interleaving where a worker thread waits while another returns a connection. The execution trace reveals that the connection successfully enters the pool, yet the waiting worker remains blocked past the notification point. This empirical finding rules out the timeout hypothesis immediately and directs the agent toward the synchronization path. The agent can then synthesize a patch that repairs the signaling logic and verify it against the failing reproducer. Passing a targeted reproducer, however, does not constitute full task acceptance; narrow tests can overlook edge cases, and neither an unverified hypothesis nor an isolated micro-test provides sufficient confidence to ship code to production. The two paths part ways before any file changes (figure 1). Acting on the first guess hides the symptom and keeps the defect, whereas checking first spends one short test to learn which hypothesis survives.

Napkin Math 3.1: A probe vs. a longer answer
Problem: A prepared diagnostic test takes 40 ms. A proposed explanation contains 1,024 output tokens. How long does the explanation take to generate relative to the test?

Variables: The illustrative per-token generation time from Per-call work estimate is 21.07 ms, derived from memory-bandwidth limits rather than a measured production service rate. The hypothetical output pricing is $15 per million tokens. Prompt processing, queueing, transport, test preparation, and the downstream model call that reads the test observation are excluded.

Math: Generating 1,024 tokens at 21.07 ms per token requires 21.58 s and incurs $0.01536 in token charges. Dividing this generation latency by the 40 ms test run yields a factor of 539.5×.

Systems insight: When the unresolved question concerns dynamic program behavior, the probe settles it 539.5× faster than the model can generate a speculative explanation of it, and only the probe’s answer is an observation. Running a test is preferable whenever its preparation and execution time remain below the explanation’s 21.58 s and its outcome decisively discriminates between competing failure modes. Conversely, an inconclusive test that does not discriminate the competing hypotheses provides no diagnostic gain regardless of its speed. Failure to reproduce can still supply negative evidence when the test’s sensitivity to the suspected fault is known. A well-designed runtime allocates work to whichever operation most efficiently eliminates the primary source of uncertainty.

The probe, and the decision to revise the patch on its evidence, both happened outside any single model call. The invocation contract of the previous chapter ends at one prompt and one candidate, accompanied only by metadata indicating whether generation reached a stop token or an output limit. That interface cannot evaluate whether allocating the next second of latency or fraction of a cent toward extended reasoning, candidate sampling, or runtime probing would yield a higher-quality fix. An API response signaling completion merely confirms that generation finished, not that the proposed patch resolves the underlying defect. Test-time compute is all the system work orchestrated around the model call, with model weights held fixed, to explore, evaluate, and verify candidates before committing to an action. An effective agentic architecture requires a principled runtime policy to govern this expenditure: establishing hard ceilings on latency and financial cost, routing compute across reasoning and validation tools, and terminating search cleanly without conflating an exhausted resource quota with a verified solution.

↩︎ The bounded single-invocation model contract is established in The Invocation Contract.

Each part of that policy raises the next question. The runtime first chooses how to spend extra work across reasoning depth, candidate breadth, and empirical feedback (section 3.2). Breadth produces a pool, so the runtime must then pick from it without being fooled by a plausible failure (section 3.3), and picking well depends on how far a passing check can be trusted (section 3.4). When a check refutes a premise partway through a plan, the runtime should regenerate only what depended on it (section 3.5). Candidates and revisions together form a search whose spending must be tracked, reserved, and stopped (section 3.6), and the complete policy is finally judged against its alternatives under one budget and one deadline (section 3.7).

3.2 Three Compute Allocation Axes

When diagnosing and fixing a concurrency bug in a connection pool, an agent system can unroll an exhaustive step-by-step derivation, sample several candidate patches in parallel, or iterate through an edit–test–revise loop. While all three strategies can consume an identical token allowance, they impose fundamentally different execution topologies. A detailed derivation must generate tokens sequentially along a single causal chain. Independent candidate patches can execute concurrently across accelerator hardware. An iterative revision cycle that incorporates test results must pause generation until diagnostic execution finishes. These structural dependencies dictate which operations lie on the critical latency path and how much state the serving infrastructure must retain in memory.

Depth extends sequential computation within a single candidate, breadth explores multiple candidate paths concurrently, and feedback incorporates environment observations into subsequent revisions. A test-time deliberation rollout represents one bounded candidate-generation path, potentially interleaving verification checks and observation-conditioned revisions; the term borrows from game search, where a rollout plays one line of moves forward to its end. Depth lengthens a rollout, breadth runs several, and feedback inserts checks between a rollout’s turns. An allocation policy can compose these axes. It might, for example, generate several candidate patches in parallel, run automated regression suites against each, and extend only the most promising candidate with step-by-step reasoning. Throughout this process, model weights remain frozen; every axis draws against a shared test-time allowance.

Definition 3.1: Test-time compute budget

Test-time compute budget is the per-decision allowance of processed prompt tokens, generated tokens, model invocations, verification checks, and elapsed wall-clock time that a runtime may expend, with model weights fixed, before it must select a candidate, abstain, or escalate to a human operator.

  1. Significance: The budget sets the attempt cost in the effective cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\) (equation 1.2). Additional deliberation reduces cost per accepted task when it increases the acceptance rate \(\AcceptanceProbability\) proportionally faster than it increases the expected attempt cost. A higher cost per accepted task can still be justified when it buys the required acceptance rate within the deadline (section 3.7.3).
  2. Distinction: Training compute amortizes weight optimization once across all future tasks. Test-time compute recurs on every decision, where an internal reasoning allowance is merely one control surface alongside external tool invocations and verification checks.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the maximum allocation ceiling as the expected spend, or treating an exhausted budget as evidence that the best remaining candidate is acceptable.

What separates the three axes is their dependency structure (figure 2). Depth extends a single candidate through multi-step reasoning, breadth fans out to sampled candidates that converge on a selector, and feedback routes a candidate through a sandboxed check whose observation conditions the revision.

3.2.1 Sequential extension along the depth axis

Depth allows an autoregressive model to unroll intermediate reasoning tokens before emitting its final decision or artifact, generalizing early scratchpad techniques and chain-of-thought prompting (Nye et al. 2021; Wei et al. 2022). In the connection-pool scenario, these intermediate tokens might systematically trace thread interleavings: acquiring the pool mutex, detecting leaked socket descriptors, and signaling waiter condition variables. Because autoregressive generation conditions on all prior tokens, later decoding steps can inspect earlier derivations, detect flawed assumptions, and alter course before outputting code. This reasoning advantage arises entirely from executing additional forward passes over the prompt context rather than modifying the underlying network weights.

Nye, Maxwell, Anders Johan Andreassen, Guy Gur-Ari, Michal Michalewski, Jacob Austin, David Bieber, David Dohan, et al. 2021. “Show Your Work: Scratchpads for Intermediate Computation with Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2112.00114, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2112.00114.

From a systems perspective, generation remains strictly autoregressive. Extending a single generation path serializes the computation. Because transformer decoding at small batch sizes is memory-bandwidth bound, step latency is governed by the time required to stream all model parameters from high-bandwidth memory into processor caches (principle 2). Consequently, wall-clock generation time scales approximately linearly with the number of generated tokens under a fixed serving configuration. A lengthy reasoning trace demonstrates only that the accelerator performed more computation, not that the proposed code compiles or executes correctly. The runtime must also leave enough output budget for the final usable artifact. A derivation that exhausts the budget mid-generation returns a truncated response, which the invocation contract quarantines like any other incomplete call rather than treating it as a candidate.

3.2.2 Parallel sampling along the breadth axis

Breadth allocates test-time compute across alternative candidate paths. Instead of extending one trajectory, the runtime requests multiple candidate patches from the same source excerpt and task specification, using stochastic decoding controls. Conditional on a fixed prompt and independent random seeds, these samples are statistically independent draws from the model’s output distribution. However, they remain bounded by the model’s underlying knowledge: if the model assigns negligible probability mass to a correct thread-notification pattern, every independent sample may reproduce the same missing broadcast. Independent draws can easily fail in identical ways.

↩︎ Temperature, top-\(\Glyph{k}\), and nucleus sampling controls are established in Sampling controls.

Because parallel candidates share no sequential dependencies, breadth exposes fine-grained concurrency. On modern accelerator hardware, this concurrency comes at minimal latency cost. Under memory-bandwidth-bound decoding (principle 2), a batch of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) concurrent candidate streams reads the model weights once per step for all \(\CandidatePoolSize\) tokens. Spending an allocation of \(\CandidatePoolSize \times \OutputLength\) tokens sequentially along the depth axis multiplies the generation latency by roughly \(\CandidatePoolSize\). In contrast, generating \(\CandidatePoolSize\) concurrent candidates of length \(\OutputLength\) in parallel keeps total generation time near the duration of the longest candidate, \(\max_n \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_n)\). This latency benefit holds as long as the concurrent batch remains memory-bandwidth bound and fits within accelerator memory.

Accelerator memory is where breadth extracts its price. Each active generation stream must maintain its own key-value (KV) cache for the tokens it generates. When the serving system implements prefix sharing, the \(\SequenceLength\)-token prompt is stored once in memory, and the peak KV cache demand scales as

\[ \BreadthPeakKvCacheMemory \propto \SequenceLength + \sum_{n=1}^{\CandidatePoolSize} \OutputLength_n \]

Without shared prefix caching, the serving engine stores \(\CandidatePoolSize\) redundant copies of the prompt’s KV tensors, causing memory demand to scale as \(\CandidatePoolSize \SequenceLength + \sum_n \OutputLength_n\). When aggregate KV cache allocations exceed hardware capacity, the serving engine must partition candidates into sequential execution waves. Once forced into waves, breadth latency degrades from the duration of the single longest candidate toward the sum of all candidates, while memory thrashing and queueing delays increase individual token latencies. Whether identical prompt prefixes share physical memory or receive provider billing discounts depends on the serving engine’s implementation.

↪ Block sharing, which stores a common prompt’s KV state once for every candidate that branches from it, is developed in Copy-on-write prefix sharing.

Breadth also shifts the operational burden downstream: generating a wide pool of candidates pays off only if the system can accurately select the best option or synthesize their differences. A candidate pool containing an optimal patch provides no value without an effective selector. In a study of repeated sampling, Brown et al. (2024) showed that while the probability of at least one candidate being correct (pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)) continued to rise across four orders of magnitude of samples, practical selection heuristics such as majority voting and learned reward models (scorers trained to predict whether a candidate is correct, examined in section 3.4.1) plateaued after several hundred samples. Candidate selection is therefore an active consumer of compute and verification budget rather than an incidental final step.

3.2.3 Observation-conditioned revision along the feedback axis

The feedback axis incorporates external observations into subsequent model requests. Rather than relying solely on frozen weights and static inputs, the repair agent generates a candidate patch, the runtime executes a bounded test suite, and the subsequent model invocation receives concrete diagnostics, such as a stack trace from a deadlocked worker thread. These empirical observations constrain subsequent candidate generation far more effectively than prompting the model to re-examine the original prompt in isolation (Shinn et al. 2023).

Shinn, Noah, Federico Cassano, Ashwin Gopinath, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 8634–52.

Verification checks yield structured outcomes: failure, success, or inconclusive execution (such as a test timeout). A failure trace pinpoints specific regression bugs or unmet invariants; a passing test confirms that the code satisfies the exercised assertions under tested conditions; and final acceptance remains a distinct policy gate. Executing diagnostics constitutes an active external action. It consumes CPU and memory resources, runs within sandboxed security boundaries, and incurs wall-clock latency even when performed purely for internal deliberation.

Each feedback round is also one more turn of the horizon \(\TurnHorizon\), which ties this axis to horizon compounding (equation 1.1). Unchecked turns compound error as \((1-\PerStepErrorRate)^{\TurnHorizon}\), and the introduction showed (notebook 1.1) that a check which rejects a bad step and retries it resets that compounding. Feedback is the one axis that places such a check inside every turn, so it can lengthen a deliberation without the unchecked compounding its extra turns would otherwise carry, to the extent that its checks deserve trust (section 3.4).

Feedback introduces strict serialization: a revision cannot begin until the preceding verification check completes and returns its observation payload. Furthermore, context policies that append prior candidates and diagnostic outputs cause input token counts to expand across successive turns. For an initial prompt length \(\BasePromptLength\), candidate lengths \(\OutputLength_j\), and observation lengths \(\lvert \TrajectoryObservation_j\rvert\), an append-only policy yields an input length at turn \(\TurnIndex\) of

\[\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)} = \BasePromptLength + \sum_{j=1}^{\TurnIndex-1}(\OutputLength_j + \lvert \TrajectoryObservation_j\rvert)\]

This is the append-only growth that equation 2.9 priced across a whole trajectory, now confined to one decision, and it inflates prompt prefill latency, accelerator memory usage, and token billing on every round. Although context-compaction techniques can summarize earlier attempts or drop obsolete diagnostic traces, the allocation policy must account for the full token volume dispatched to the model API.

↪ Context-compaction methods and their scheduling within the request budget are developed in Context Compaction.

3.2.4 The three axes under one output ceiling

Runtimes schedule these three allocation axes against four resource dimensions: input and output token counts, critical-path wall-clock latency, peak retained accelerator state, and external information acquisition. Let \(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength)\) be the time required to ingest an \(\SequenceLength\)-token prompt, \(\PerTokenDecodeTime\) the per-token decoding step time, \(\DecodeInterval(\OutputLength)\) the total generation time for \(\OutputLength\) tokens, and \(\FeedbackCheckDuration\) the duration of the external check in feedback round \(\TurnIndex\), which is also the turn index of the trajectory. With \(\ExtendedDepthLength\) tokens of extended depth, \(\CandidatePoolSize\) breadth candidates executed concurrently, and \(\TurnHorizon\) feedback turns, the latency dimension decomposes into three critical paths:

\[ \begin{aligned} \DepthCriticalPathLatency &\approx \PrefillTime(\SequenceLength) + \ExtendedDepthLength\,\PerTokenDecodeTime,\\ \BreadthCriticalPathLatency &\approx \PrefillTime(\SequenceLength) + \max_{n} \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_n),\\ \FeedbackCriticalPathLatency &\approx \sum_{t=1}^{\TurnHorizon}\bigl(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)}) + \DecodeInterval(\OutputLength_t) + {\FeedbackCheckDuration}_{\TurnIndex}\bigr). \end{aligned} \tag{1}\]

The critical path for breadth assumes the serving infrastructure maintains sufficient memory and execution capacity to decode all \(\CandidatePoolSize\) streams concurrently at an unchanged step time. When capacity saturates, decoding falls back into serialized waves, shifting the critical path toward the sum across candidate streams. In contrast, the feedback expression grows along two distinct dimensions: sequential check durations \(\FeedbackCheckDuration\) and lengthening prompt prefill times \(\PrefillTime(\SequenceLength^{(\TurnIndex)})\) caused by context accumulation. The equation covers only latency. Table 1 sets those critical paths beside the other three dimensions, the tokens processed, the peak model state retained, and the external information acquired.

Among the three topologies, only feedback admits external information absent from the model weights and prompt. The code below models these expressions for the connection-pool repair under a fixed output budget of 4,096 tokens, isolating how identical output ceilings generate starkly different latency profiles and monetary bills.

Napkin Math 3.2: One decision, three allocations
Problem: A repair request starts with 4,096 input tokens and a common ceiling of 4,096 generated tokens. How do depth, breadth, and feedback spend that allowance?

Variables: Reuse the illustrative per-token generation time from the preceding example. Breadth has capacity for 4 concurrent streams at that same rate. Each short candidate uses 1,024 generated tokens. Feedback uses 3 turns, each followed by a 1.5 s check returning 340 tokens. Later feedback requests append earlier candidates and reports. Hypothetical prices are $3 per million input tokens and $15 per million output tokens, without cache discounts, so each breadth stream is billed for its own copy of the prompt (unlike the shared-prefix row of table 1). Generation times omit prefill, queueing, and transport; the feedback time additionally includes its checks. The bill counts model tokens only, and table 2 tallies each allocation’s tokens, time, and bill side by side.

Math: In table 2, depth generates all 4,096 tokens along a single path. Breadth generates 1,024 tokens on each of 4 concurrent paths, shortening the critical path by 4×. However, reading the prompt 4 times across unshared streams adds 12,288 input tokens, multiplying its billing cost to 1.5× that of depth. Feedback accumulates input context over \(\TurnHorizon\) turns according to \(\TurnHorizon\,\BasePromptLength + (\OutputLength + \lvert \TrajectoryObservation\rvert)\,\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)/2\), reaching 16,380 tokens at \(\TurnHorizon =\) 3 because each successive round re-reads all prior candidates and observation reports.

Systems insight: Breadth trades monetary cost for reduced wall-clock latency, where the entire billing premium stems from the \((\CandidatePoolSize-1)\SequenceLength\) re-read prompt tokens. The key governing lever is the ratio of prompt length to generated output: a large repository context makes breadth’s financial overhead scale aggressively with both \(\CandidatePoolSize\) and \(\SequenceLength\), whereas a short prompt or a shared prefix cache eliminates most of this penalty. Feedback achieves an intermediate balance between latency and billing cost while remaining the only topology capable of acquiring empirical ground truth. Because this resource accounting excludes solution success rates, it models infrastructural cost rather than output quality.

3.2.5 Internal budgets and external orchestration

Production systems operate across two distinct control planes: internal model deliberation and external workflow orchestration. A model service may expose an internal reasoning budget, while the runtime independently governs how many calls to dispatch, which tool checks to invoke, and what context to supply in subsequent requests. These represent different control surfaces. An internal budget governs the reasoning depth permitted within a single model invocation; external orchestration directs breadth across candidate branches, schedules sandboxed verification runs, and constructs revised prompts. Because commercial APIs often hide or redact intermediate reasoning traces, the runtime cannot evaluate computational effort by inspecting output text lengths. Instead, it must rely on explicit token usage headers and invocation status codes to enforce quotas and track system cost.

3.2.6 Reasoning models and generation allowances

Reasoning models undergo reinforcement learning on tasks with objective, verifiable outcomes, such as formal mathematics, logic puzzles, and unit-tested codebases. Through this training, they learn to produce extended intermediate scratchpads that propose solutions, verify intermediate steps, and backtrack within a single generation pass (OpenAI 2024; DeepSeek-AI et al. 2025). For system designers, this architecture introduces three core operational implications:

OpenAI. 2024. Learning to Reason with LLMs. OpenAI Research Blog.
  1. Token metering: Reasoning tokens are standard autoregressive output tokens. They consume accelerator compute, populate the KV cache, and incur standard decoding charges, regardless of whether the user interface displays them or redacts them behind summary metadata.
  2. Dynamic depth control: A reasoning-budget parameter transforms sequential depth from a static model property into a dynamic, per-request runtime allocation. The orchestration layer can calibrate this knob on a per-task basis.
  3. Knowledge boundaries: Internal reasoning operates strictly over the supplied prompt and static network weights. An extended trace can verify arithmetic or check internal logic consistency, but it cannot observe external execution behavior. It cannot determine whether a proposed patch resolves a connection leak without running an actual test suite.

↪ Reinforcement learning on tasks with verifiable outcomes, the training that produces reasoning models, is developed in Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards.

Because models trained with verifiable rewards use generation allowances differently than instruction-tuned models (models adapted to follow example instructions and responses), budget calibration is highly model-dependent. Furthermore, when internal reasoning traces remain hidden, the runtime can inspect only its own external invocation telemetry and verification results. These records guide the optimization of orchestration policies rather than providing formal guarantees of candidate correctness.

Treating depth as a per-call budget allows the runtime to allocate tokens dynamically across tasks. The core engineering objective is determining what each compute allocation topology yields across tasks of varying intrinsic difficulty.

Example 3.1: Compute-optimal test-time allocation
Scenario: Snell et al. (2024) evaluated strategies for spending a fixed test-time compute budget across reasoning benchmarks, comparing sequential revision of a single candidate against parallel breadth sampling and search guided by learned process verifiers (section 3.4). The study partitioned benchmark problems into difficulty tiers based on the base model’s zero-shot success rate.

Diagnosis: On simpler problems, sequential revision used the compute budget most effectively, refining minor errors without the overhead of parallel sampling. On moderately difficult problems, hybrid strategies combining parallel exploration with guided search achieved higher accuracy. On the hardest problems, however, scaling test-time compute yielded diminishing returns across all allocation axes. When evaluated at matched total FLOPs, a compute-optimal test-time allocation enabled a smaller model to outperform a model 14 times larger, provided the smaller model possessed a nonzero base success rate. When base success hovered near zero, allocating FLOPs to a larger, more capable base model proved far more effective than exhausting tokens on parallel rollouts.

Systems lesson: With the supplied information held fixed, additional reasoning and sampling can elicit nonzero baseline capabilities but cannot acquire absent environmental facts. Feedback can supply new observations to the next call, whose value still depends on the model’s ability to use them and the check’s coverage. Runtimes should dynamically route compute budgets by estimated task difficulty: allocate modest budgets or single candidates to straightforward tasks, deploy parallel search to moderately complex tasks, and escalate near-zero confidence requests directly to larger models or human supervisors rather than wasting tokens on unguided sampling.

Because an execution engine cannot directly observe intrinsic task difficulty a priori, it must approximate difficulty using low-latency, low-cost heuristics before committing an expensive deliberation budget. Practical heuristics include consensus among two or three low-temperature pilot samples, the output of fast static analysis linters, and historical error rates across similar task classes. When pilot signals demonstrate high consensus or pass static checks cleanly, the runtime allocates a minimal token budget to a single candidate. When pilot samples diverge or linters flag structural ambiguities, the scheduler escalates the request to a broader search or an iterative feedback loop. If a decision remains unresolved after exhausting a bounded budget ceiling, the runtime halts deliberation and escalates to a human operator rather than burning additional tokens on low-probability convergence. Each routing heuristic acts as a predictive classifier whose precision and recall must be empirically validated against held-out task distributions.

In production agent workflows, internal reasoning depth and external search breadth naturally compose. A single deliberation rollout may use enough depth to generate a structurally coherent, well-reasoned code patch; the runtime then samples several such reasoned candidates across the breadth axis and filters them through an automated test harness along the feedback axis. The composition must still be tuned to real system bottlenecks. Slow tool execution inflates feedback latency along the critical path, whereas imprecise test suites diminish the informational value of each feedback observation.

The checks that make this composition work matter beyond inference. The validation checks a runtime uses to accept candidates at test time are the same kind of check that reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards turns into a training reward. An edge case, false pass, or specification gap in a test-time acceptance check therefore costs one bad selection at inference, but once the check becomes a reward, training searches for that gap on every update.

↪ How an acceptance check becomes a training reward, and how its gaps are exploited during training, is developed in Verifiable Rewards.

Checkpoint 3.1: Choosing an allocation

Allocating compute across depth, breadth, and feedback defines where extra computation is spent, but generation alone does not determine which candidate should survive. Even an allocation that discovers a correct repair fails if downstream selection favors a plausible incorrect alternative. The remaining challenge is candidate evaluation: comparing competing proposals while accounting for the costs, latencies, and blind spots of the verification mechanisms that judge them.

3.3 Candidate Selection

Generating a broad candidate pool gives an agent a portfolio of proposals, but breadth alone does not solve a task. In a connection-pool repair task, for example, one candidate might lengthen an acquisition timeout, another might inject exponential retries, and a third might alter how the connection-return path notifies waiting threads. The runtime must determine which proposal, if any, carries sufficient supporting evidence to return, and evaluating that evidence consumes execution time and tokens from the same deliberation budget that generated the candidates.

Two distinct requirements separate generation from selection: whether the candidate pool contains at least one solution that satisfies the task requirements, and whether the selector can identify that valid solution without mistakenly accepting an unsuitable one. A generation policy can flood the pool with candidate solutions without making selection any easier. Conversely, a more discerning selector can dramatically raise the reliability of the system without sampling a single extra candidate.

3.3.1 Useful variation among candidates

Syntactic variation does not guarantee algorithmic diversity. Renaming local variables, reformatting comments, or rewriting an iteration loop can produce completely distinct token sequences while leaving a broken synchronization bug completely untouched. For our repair task, candidate alternatives provide practical value only when they explore distinct hypotheses or failure modes that downstream checks can actually differentiate. Adjusting thread notification logic vs. extending a timeout explores two different failure hypotheses; generating several slightly reworded explanations for the same timeout adjustment simply wastes verification cycles.

This failure mode is distinct from statistical dependence. When the task, prompt, base model, and sampling policy remain fixed, separate generation passes driven by independent random seeds are genuinely independent and identically distributed (IID). However, if the model concentrates nearly all its probability mass on timeout adjustments, independent sampling predictably floods the pool with variations of that single change.

Across a heterogeneous production workload, common model limitations become even more pronounced. A model typically solves standard, well-represented patterns reliably while failing consistently on obscure edge cases. Consequently, repeated attempts on a difficult task fail together far more frequently than an aggregate benchmark pass rate would imply. While individual samples on a single fixed task may be conditionally independent, treating attempts across a mixed workload as draws with one common success probability obscures this correlation. Feedback-conditioned generation further alters the distribution: when the runtime feeds execution errors back into subsequent prompts, later candidates become explicitly dependent on what earlier checks revealed.

A runtime can encourage diversity by adjusting sampling parameters or prompting the model to explore distinct diagnostic angles under the original problem constraints. However, aggressive diversification carries real engineering risks: unconstrained sampling often introduces malformed code, nonsensical approaches, or outright violations of baseline invariants. The test for any diversification strategy is whether it improves task success under a fixed compute and latency budget.

To conserve verification bandwidth, the runtime should prune redundant candidates early using structural and behavioral deduplication. Abstract syntax tree (AST) normalization strips away superficial differences such as variable names and whitespace. Behavioral clustering then evaluates candidates against common test inputs to detect identical runtime behavior. This filtering allowed a competitive code-generation system to condense tens of thousands of raw model outputs into a handful of viable submissions (Li et al. 2022). Both techniques establish bounded equivalence relations, whether syntactic equivalence under canonicalization or behavioral agreement over a test suite. Such deduplication is sound whenever that relation preserves the specific property being evaluated.

Li, Yujia, David Choi, Junyoung Chung, Nate Kushman, Julian Schrittwieser, Rémi Leblond, Tom Eccles, et al. 2022. “Competition-Level Code Generation with AlphaCode.” Science 378 (6624): 1092–97. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.abq1158.

3.3.2 Selection policies

A runtime evaluates candidates using three primary mechanisms: direct execution checks, answer agreement (consensus voting), and learned ranking. System designers often combine these approaches, tailoring the pipeline to the available evidence and the cost of evaluation rather than treating them as fixed, sequential stages.

Direct checks evaluate a candidate deterministically against explicit specifications. A parser rejects malformed syntax; a type checker enforces interface contracts and memory invariants; an automated test harness measures dynamic behavior on specified inputs. Each check operates within a strictly defined domain: successful compilation proves structural validity but says nothing about whether a race condition was resolved, while a regression test validates only the execution paths covered by its test fixtures. Furthermore, candidate execution remains a governed action that requires sandbox isolation, strict timeouts, and privilege boundaries appropriate to the environment.

Answer agreement aggregates independent reasoning paths through consensus. In self-consistency, the runtime samples multiple reasoning trajectories and selects the plurality answer (Wang et al. 2023). For arithmetic, symbolic logic, or standardized factual queries, the system normalizes the answer representation and takes the majority vote. However, consensus is merely a property of the sampled distribution: when a model harbors a systematic bias or flawed reasoning shortcut, multiple independent paths frequently converge on the exact same error. In software repairs, textual voting is a poor comparison rule; agreement on observed behaviors carries the test coverage limits just described, and a correct minority candidate deserves an execution check rather than being outvoted by plausible failures.

When deterministic checks cannot fully validate candidate quality, learned rankers and model-based judges provide heuristic evaluation. A learned reward model or a secondary large language model (LLM) judge (a model prompted to grade another model’s output) can evaluate explanatory adequacy, score style, or prioritize promising patches before invoking costly regression suites. Nevertheless, a learned ranking remains a statistical estimate whose relevance must be evaluated on the target task distribution. A fundamental distinction exists between ranking candidates and accepting a solution. Ranking merely orders candidates from best to worst according to a scoring function; acceptance determines whether a candidate satisfies the operational requirements of the task. If an entire candidate pool is defective, selecting the highest-ranked member simply returns the best failure. Validating acceptance requires an objective verifier that yields an authoritative pass or fail verdict.

Definition 3.2: Verifier-guided selection

Verifier-guided selection is a selection policy that returns a candidate only when a verifier, a check whose criteria and observations the candidate cannot modify, passes it, and abstains when no candidate passes.

  1. Significance: Its success rate is capped by pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), the chance the pool holds a passing candidate (section 3.3.3), while the share of its selections that are correct depends on both the verifier’s passing-set precision and the selection policy (section 3.4.2). The fixed IID first-passing policy developed there preserves passing-set precision; ranking among passing candidates need not. Selection coverage and selected correctness together determine the probability of returning a valid result.
  2. Distinction: Voting selects what the generator produces most often and learned ranking selects what a scorer prefers; only a verifier’s verdict can justify returning a candidate instead of abstaining.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the verifier’s pass as task acceptance; the verdict covers the checked property, and its false acceptance rate grows in importance as the pool’s valid-candidate rate falls.

The hypothetical repair pool in table 3 illustrates how these distinct verification stages expose candidate quality. The checks are deliberately bounded: a focused reproducer exercises the known race condition, while broader regression suites remain untested.

A production runtime pipelines these checks to maximize rejection efficiency, ordering them by cost and filtering power rather than arbitrary conceptual categories. A lightweight structural check that parses syntax or validates an AST costs fractions of a millisecond and quickly discards malformed candidates, saving expensive sandboxed execution environments and high-latency LLM scoring passes from wasted work. However, pipeline ordering must reflect empirical systems profiling rather than intuition. For illustration, executing a compiled binary in a warm container might take 5 ms, whereas invoking a large model-based judge to evaluate code style is a full model call that might take 800 ms and consume a share of the token budget. Moreover, joint candidate comparison scales differently with context window size than scoring each candidate independently. Because verification overhead directly cuts into the runtime’s available time and compute, candidate selection must be budgeted as an integral component of test-time compute.

3.3.3 Candidate availability under pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)

pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) measures the probability that at least one of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) sampled candidates passes the specified tests (Chen et al. 2021).1

1 Origin of pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\): The metric was introduced to score program synthesis by functional correctness rather than by textual similarity to a reference (Kulal et al. 2019). Because plugging the observed pass fraction \(\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount\) into \(1-(1-\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount)^\IndependentAttemptCount\) biases the estimate of what breadth buys, Chen et al. (2021) replaced it with the combinatorial estimator derived below. It measures availability in the pool, not the success rate of the output a deployed selector returns.

Kulal, Sumith, Panupong Pasupat, Kartik Chandra, Mina Lee, Oded Padon, Alex Aiken, and Percy S. Liang. 2019. “SPoC: Search-Based Pseudocode to Code.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32.
Chen, Mark, Jerry Tworek, Heewoo Jun, Qiming Yuan, Henrique Ponde de Oliveira Pinto, Jared Kaplan, Harri Edwards, et al. 2021. “Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2107.03374.

For a fixed task and fixed generation policy, suppose independent samples each pass the tests with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\). The probability that all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) samples fail is \((1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), so

\[ \operatorname{pass@}\IndependentAttemptCount = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount. \]

This expression is conditional on that task and sampling policy. To summarize a workload with different task-level pass probabilities, average the task-level quantities; substituting the workload’s average \(\AttemptPassProbability\) generally gives a different result. For an adaptive policy that revises its prompt after feedback, evaluate the policy’s resulting pools rather than assuming they follow this fixed-distribution formula.

In a benchmark, generate \(\GeneratedSampleCount \ge \IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates for a task and let \(\PassingSampleCount\) pass the fixed test criterion. Of the \(\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) subsets of size \(\IndependentAttemptCount\), exactly \(\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) contain only failures. The subset estimator is therefore

\[ \PassAtKEstimate = 1-\frac{\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}{\binom{\GeneratedSampleCount}{\IndependentAttemptCount}}, \]

with the numerator taken as zero when \(\GeneratedSampleCount-\PassingSampleCount<\IndependentAttemptCount\). Under independent, identically distributed sampling for the fixed task, this estimates the probability that a fresh pool of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) candidates contains a test-passing member without bias; Pass@\IndependentAttemptCount and repeated-run consistency derives this expectation and details evaluation without factorials. Benchmark results average these estimates over tasks (Chen et al. 2021).

The relationship to deployed selection follows from the event being measured. If the selector must return an unchanged member of the same pool, it cannot return a test-passing candidate when the pool contains none. Its probability of returning a test-passing candidate is therefore at most pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), counting abstention as no returned pass. A selector that always finds a passing member whenever one exists reaches this ceiling; whether the deployed selector does, within the deployment’s checking budget, is a separate measurement.

The predicate is passing these tests, which is narrower than satisfying the complete task requirements, and the ceiling covers only selectors that return an unchanged pool member; a selector that repairs candidates or synthesizes a new one generates additional artifacts. Report candidate availability alongside selected-output performance under the relevant criterion.

The gap between the two can be large. Figure 3 recomputes the plateau described in section 3.2.2 from the released per-sample correctness labels of Brown et al. (2024), plotting coverage (the task-averaged pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) estimate) against the accuracy of majority voting over the same pools.

At 10,000 samples, the smaller model’s pools hold a correct answer for 98 percent of the problems, yet majority voting returns one for 43 percent, and 59 percent for the larger model. Beyond 256 samples the vote gains less than a point while coverage gains another 10. The repair agent’s coverage rises from 16 percent with one sample to 56 percent at 250, but that growth is usable only because tests can identify which candidate passes. Spending on breadth is therefore justified by the selector, not by the pool: without a check that recognizes the correct candidate, the added samples raise the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) ceiling without raising what the runtime returns.

↪ The distinction between pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) over a candidate pool and consistency over repeated system runs is developed in Potential and repeated-run consistency.

Each doubling squares the chance that all candidates fail, so the bars approach certainty.

Breadth has a price that pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) makes easy to compute. In best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling, the runtime generates \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates for one decision and returns one that its selector admits. The pool is the same object pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) describes, so its availability is pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) with \(\IndependentAttemptCount = \CandidatePoolSize\), written pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) from here on. The notebook below prices that pool against the simplest alternative, generating one candidate at a time and stopping at the first pass, under the most favorable selection assumption: the verifier is the acceptance check itself.

Napkin Math 3.3: The price of a larger pool
Problem: Each candidate repair passes the task’s verifier with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\), independently for this task, and the verifier is the acceptance check. How many candidates per accepted repair does a parallel pool of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) spend, compared with sequential retry that stops at the first pass and gives up after \(\CandidatePoolSize\) attempts?

Variables: \(\AttemptPassProbability\) = 30 percent (hypothetical); pools of 1, 2, 4, and 8 candidates; a selector that returns a passing member whenever one exists.

Math: The formula pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize\) gives 30, 51, 76, and 94.2 percent for those pools. The last of 8 candidates adds \(\AttemptPassProbability(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize-1}\), only 2.5 percentage points. The parallel pool always spends 8 candidates, or 8.5 per accepted repair. Sequential retry fails only when all 8 attempts fail, so it reaches the same 94.2 percent, yet it spends on average \([1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize]/\AttemptPassProbability\) = 3.14 candidates, or \(1/\AttemptPassProbability\) = 3.3 per accepted repair.

Systems insight: At equal success, the parallel pool costs 2.5× as much per accepted repair; what it buys is one round instead of an expected 3.14 sequential generate-and-check rounds. Breadth buys latency, not cost. In \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\) it raises \(\AcceptanceProbability\) per round but raises the attempt cost faster. Size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) as the smallest pool whose pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) meets the target within the rounds the deadline allows, and stop growing it once the next candidate’s gain is worth less than its cost.

3.3.4 Selection and evidence costs

Expanding candidate breadth increases the likelihood that a valid solution enters the pool, but it simultaneously inflates the verification burden. Under a fixed deliberation budget, spending all resources on generation starves the verification stage, forcing the selector to rely on shallow heuristics. A smaller pool paired with informative, high-coverage checks frequently outperforms a large pool governed by weak selection. Conversely, when generation coverage is the primary bottleneck, expanding the pool yields immediate returns. Comparing these regimes requires evaluating the entire generation-and-selection policy rather than measuring either stage in isolation.

Checker errors compound rapidly as candidate pools grow. Every additional invalid candidate introduced into the pool provides another opportunity for an imperfect verifier to register a false pass. If a verifier has a 5 percent false acceptance rate (it passes 5 percent of invalid candidates, a rate defined formally in section 3.4.2), evaluating twenty invalid proposals with independent check outcomes makes at least one false pass likely (\(1 - (1 - 0.05)^{20} \approx 64\%\)). Whether that false pass is actually returned depends on what else the pool holds and on the selection rule, a distinction that section makes exact. The ultimate error rate of the accepted solution depends on the base quality of the candidate distribution, the check’s conditional error rates, and the rule used to break ties among passing candidates. When an adaptive search algorithm steers generation based on verifier feedback, it actively shifts all three factors, often optimizing directly against the verifier’s blind spots.

Where the runtime gathers evidence dictates both the latency and the precision of selection. Validating only the final output may suffice when end-to-end execution checks are cheap and definitive. For extended multi-step trajectories, however, checking intermediate assumptions allows the runtime to prune failing branches before investing further compute. This establishes a fundamental design distinction between outcome checks that evaluate finished artifacts and process checks that govern multi-step execution.

3.4 Candidate Verification

A search policy requires evidence that separates a genuinely correct candidate from a superficially plausible one. In a connection-pool repair, for instance, clean compilation confirms only syntactic and type validity; verifying that blocked worker threads actually wake up when a connection returns requires runtime execution. Even a targeted concurrency test explores only a subset of thread interleavings and can easily miss subtle race conditions. Every validation check answers a strictly bounded question. System designers must therefore decide which questions to ask, when to schedule each evaluation, and how the resulting evidence should redirect the remaining search budget.

3.4.1 Outcome and step-level evidence

An outcome check evaluates a completed candidate against an external criterion, such as whether a patched service passes an end-to-end regression test. In contrast, an outcome reward model predicts a scalar quality score for the completed candidate, functioning like the trained verifiers used to rank candidate solutions for mathematical reasoning (Cobbe et al. 2021). The check executes the candidate and reports what happened; the reward model only predicts how good the candidate is.

2 Step-Level Labels (PRM800K): Outcome labels for code can be produced automatically by running tests. Step labels require a judgment on each intermediate step, and the PRM800K dataset used to train the process reward model of Lightman et al. (2024) contains about 800,000 human step-level labels. That labeling cost is one reason learned process scorers are scarcer than outcome checks.

Lightman, Hunter, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, Harri Edwards, Bowen Baker, Teddy Lee, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, and Karl Cobbe. 2024. “Let’s Verify Step by Step.” The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Step-level checks evaluate intermediate claims or state transitions. In our repair task, a check might verify whether a waiting worker receives a wake-up notification before the agent drafts a complex timeout adjustment. If an execution trace refutes the timeout hypothesis, the runtime can terminate that branch immediately, conserving generation tokens. Learned process scorers similarly rank or prune partial reasoning trajectories, treating low step scores as probabilistic signals of downstream failure. Process supervision2 assigns feedback to discrete intermediate steps rather than deferring evaluation to terminal outcomes (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024). The difference between outcome and process feedback lies in where the feedback attaches, not in what the evaluator may read, since a learned outcome scorer can also inspect the full reasoning trajectory before scoring the final output.

Definition 3.3: Process reward model (PRM)

A process reward model (PRM) is a learned scorer that assigns a value to each intermediate step of a candidate trajectory, conditioned on the task context and the preceding steps.

  1. Significance: Step scores allow search algorithms to prune unpromising branches early, keeping beam search and tree search, the search procedures that extend only the best-scoring partial candidates (section 3.6.1), within finite computational budgets.
  2. Distinction: An outcome reward model evaluates only terminal states; a process reward model assigns credit across intermediate steps. This early steering capability requires dense step-level supervision, which costs substantially more to gather than automated test outcomes.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking a high step score for verified correctness. A process reward model provides heuristic guidance for ranking and pruning, but it offers no formal guarantees and satisfies no closure evidence level.

Each verification granularity catches failures that the other overlooks. An outcome check evaluates global behavior, exposing subtle integration bugs that appear harmless when examined step by step. Conversely, a process check isolates errors early, preventing bad trajectories from consuming additional inference cycles. However, verifying too frequently consumes system resources and risks discarding viable candidate branches due to verifier noise. The system trade-off weighs avoided downstream generation against verification overhead and false rejections.

Computational cost also depends heavily on input scoping and prefix reuse. Consider a verifier that evaluates a task prompt of \(\SequenceLength\) tokens and the candidate’s growing prefix after each of its \(\ScoredStepCount\) steps, with each step appending \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) tokens. Evaluating each cumulative prefix from scratch, without reusing key-value cache states from preceding steps, requires processing:

\[ \sum_{j=1}^{\ScoredStepCount}\left(\SequenceLength + \ScoredStepIndex\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\right) = \ScoredStepCount \SequenceLength + \frac{\ScoredStepCount(\ScoredStepCount+1)}{2}\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength \tag{2}\]

scorer input tokens, an overhead that scales quadratically with trajectory length \(\ScoredStepCount\). The beam-search estimate later in this chapter (notebook 3.6) bills the \(\ScoredStepIndex\,\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) term of equation 2, with the task prompt \(\SequenceLength\) excluded, for every extension it scores at step \(\ScoredStepIndex\). Incremental evaluation eliminates this quadratic burden when the serving engine supports prefix caching, reducing the marginal per-step cost to \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\) tokens. Evaluators that require the complete candidate or lack cache-aware serving pay the full quadratic cost. Verification scheduling must therefore account directly for prompt length, caching efficiency, and invocation latency.

Table 4 compares four common classes of checks by their inspection scope, failure modes, and deterministic guarantees, mapping each to the closure evidence levels defined in The invariant closure principle. Compilers and sealed test suites establish bounded, reproducible properties within their domains, whereas learned reward models serve strictly as heuristic ranking instruments.

Selecting an effective check requires matching the observer’s coverage directly to the failure modes that the application cannot tolerate.

3.4.2 Check precision and the candidate pool

Structural validity does not guarantee task success. In search systems, the central reliability question is what fraction of the candidates that satisfy a given check truly solves the task. Define the event \(\text{Valid}\) as meeting the task specification and \(\text{PASS}\) as receiving a positive verifier verdict. Let \(\ValidCandidateRate = \Pr(\text{Valid})\) denote the baseline rate of valid candidates within the candidate generator’s distribution, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = \Pr(\text{PASS}\mid\neg\text{Valid})\) the false acceptance rate, and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = \Pr(\neg\text{PASS}\mid\text{Valid})\) the false rejection rate. In this formulation, “acceptance” denotes an internal verifier outcome rather than an authorization to deploy code to production. The valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate\) is distinct from the test-pass probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) used in pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\). One concerns meeting the task specification; the other concerns passing the stated tests. An error-free check makes the two events coincide. Separating them is the point of this section.

The marginal probability of a positive verification result is \(\VerifierPassProbability=\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)+(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\). Applying Bayes’ rule yields the precision of positive verdicts:

\[ \VerifierPrecision \equiv \Pr(\text{Valid}\mid\text{PASS}) =\frac{\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)}{\ValidCandidateRate(1-\VerifierFalseRejectionRate)+(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate}. \tag{3}\]

At a low valid rate, even a strict check passes many invalid candidates.

When the base rate of valid candidates is low, even a verifier with an apparently modest false acceptance rate produces a passing pool dominated by invalid proposals, the classic base-rate fallacy. The foundation-model chapter applied the same Bayes arithmetic to one checked patch, where a fallible checker barely moved the defect share (systems perspective 2.1); equation 3 is its complement, applied to every candidate in a pool. This is the verification asymmetry (principle 3) in quantitative form. Producing a valid candidate and establishing that a passing candidate is valid are different events, and the base rate and the check’s error rates, not the size of the pool, bound how far a pass can be trusted. Engineers must therefore measure error rates empirically for each target task, model family, and verification environment.

While equation 3 governs the population of passing candidates, translating this metric into the expected reliability of the selected candidate requires specifying a selection policy. Consider independent and identically distributed candidate-check pairs for a given task, evaluated under fixed error rates. The runtime generates a batch, evaluates candidates in an arbitrary order independent of quality, and selects the first candidate that passes, abstaining if none succeeds. Under these assumptions, the precision conditional on making a selection equals \(\VerifierPrecision\). Evaluating candidates in sequence reveals only that prior candidates failed; under independence, this search history does not alter the probability that the first passing candidate is valid. Conversely, selecting the candidate with the highest continuous reward score actively concentrates verifier measurement errors, a failure mode analyzed in section 3.4.3.

Napkin Math 3.4: Yield and risk under asymmetric verifier error
Problem: A repair policy generates all 64 candidates before selection. It uses the first-passing rule above and abstains when no candidate passes. What does additional breadth buy, and what risk remains?

Variables: All rates are hypothetical. Each candidate contains 1,024 tokens, billed at $15 per million output tokens. The valid-candidate rate is 5 percent, false rejection is 10 percent, and false acceptance is 2 percent. Suppose a separately authorized deployment policy deploys the selected candidate without further checking. A defective deployment incurs $500 in incident loss. For this calculation alone, valid deployments and abstentions incur zero incident loss, and missed repairs and delay are left unpriced.

Math: The expected passing counts are 2.88 valid candidates and 1.216 invalid candidates, totaling 4.096. Equation 3 yields 70.31 percent precision. Conditional on making a selection, the probability of a defective selection is therefore 29.69 percent, with expected incident loss $148.44 per selected deployment.

The probability of any selection is \(1-(1-\VerifierPassProbability)^\CandidatePoolSize\), or 98.55 percent; the policy abstains on 1.45 percent of batches. Multiplying selection coverage by conditional defect probability gives 29.26 percent defective selections per task attempt and $146.28 expected incident loss per attempt, including abstentions. Generating the entire batch costs $0.98 in output charges. Input charges, checking, and other operational costs are omitted. A sequential policy that stops generation at the first pass would have a different generation bill.

Systems insight: Expected incident loss per attempt is about 149 times the output bill, so the acceptance policy, not the token price, sets the cost of this task. Precision responds to two knobs of similar strength: halving the false acceptance rate raises it from 70.31 percent to 82.6 percent, and doubling the valid-candidate rate raises it to 83.3 percent. More candidates move neither. At a low base rate, spend on whichever of a better generator or a stricter check is cheaper, and price false rejections too when a missed valid repair is costly.

Holding \(\ValidCandidateRate\), \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\), and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) fixed, increasing the batch size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) does not improve precision \(\VerifierPrecision\). Expanding candidate breadth raises the probability of surfacing at least one passing candidate, but it simultaneously compounds the likelihood that the batch contains at least one false acceptance: \(1-[1-(1-\ValidCandidateRate)\,\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate]^\CandidatePoolSize\). In our numerical scenario, that false-pass exposure reaches 70.70 percent, whereas the actual probability of deploying a defective candidate is 29.26 percent. Under a first-passing policy, a batch can contain invalid passes without consequence as long as an earlier valid candidate is evaluated and accepted first. System designers must distinguish candidate yield, verifier precision, and end-to-end deployment risk.

3.4.3 Selection pressure on imperfect checks

The static distribution model serves as an engineering baseline, but adaptive search shifts the underlying distribution. A search policy that iteratively refines candidates to maximize an evaluation score alters the candidate population, driving proposals into regions where the evaluator is poorly calibrated. Under sustained selection pressure, the initial estimates of \(\ValidCandidateRate\), \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\), and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) no longer characterize the candidates that reach final evaluation. Optimization against imperfect proxy metrics degrades performance through distinct failure mechanisms (Manheim and Garrabrant 2018). Manheim and Garrabrant formalize these phenomena as variants of Goodhart’s law,3 which observes that any metric optimized as a target loses its validity as a measurement. Three variants directly impact agentic systems: regressional Goodhart occurs when an argmax operator selects for positive measurement noise; extremal Goodhart emerges when correlations observed across typical candidates break down in the distributional tails explored by search; and adversarial Goodhart occurs when candidate code actively manipulates or bypasses the evaluation harness.

Manheim, David, and Scott Garrabrant. 2018. “Categorizing Goodhart’s Law.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1803.04585.

3 Goodhart’s Law: The economist Charles Goodhart stated it in 1975 about monetary policy: any observed statistical regularity tends to collapse once pressure is placed upon it for control purposes (Goodhart 1984). The anthropologist Marilyn Strathern later gave it the form usually quoted today, that when a measure becomes a target it ceases to be a good measure (Strathern 1997). A test suite used to select patches is such a regularity, a reliable description of past candidates that weakens once search optimizes against it.

Goodhart, Charles A. E. 1984. “Problems of Monetary Management: The UK Experience.” In Monetary Theory and Practice. Palgrave. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-349-17295-5_4.
Strathern, Marilyn. 1997. “‘Improving Ratings’: Audit in the British University System.” European Review 5 (3): 305–21. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1234-981X(199707)5:3<305::AID-EURO184>3.0.CO;2-4.

The regressional mechanism can be formulated analytically. Suppose a learned scorer reports an estimated utility \(\EstimatedCandidateUtility_i = \CandidateUtility_i + \CandidateScoringError_i\) for candidate \(\CandidateIndex\), where \(\CandidateUtility_i\) represents genuine task utility and \(\CandidateScoringError_i\) represents an independent scoring error with zero mean and standard deviation \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\) (scoring noise, unrelated to the per-turn error rate of horizon compounding). Selecting \(\arg\max_i \EstimatedCandidateUtility_i\) maximizes the combined sum, meaning the chosen candidate is disproportionately one that benefited from an unusually large positive error. For independent Gaussian errors, the expected maximum error among \(\CandidatePoolSize\) proposals scales asymptotically as:

\[ \mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{i \le \CandidatePoolSize} \CandidateScoringError_i\Bigr] \approx \ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\sqrt{2\ln \CandidatePoolSize} \tag{4}\]

for large \(\CandidatePoolSize\).4 Expressed in units of \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\), the exact numerical expectation is 1.0 for 4 candidates and reaches 2.3 for 64 candidates, where the asymptotic expression predicts 2.9. This expected maximum is an upper bound on the selected candidate’s expected scoring error, attained when all candidates have equal true utility. When true utilities vary, selection depends on both utility and noise; Exercise 8 derives the selected error for one such distribution. An acceptance threshold calibrated on individual, unselected completions therefore requires re-evaluation on selected candidates, because argmax selection can concentrate positive verifier errors.

4 Extreme-Value Asymptotics: The form \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation\sqrt{2\ln \CandidatePoolSize}\) is only the leading term of the Gaussian maximum, and convergence is slow, which is why the chapter computes the exact expectation beside it. The Gaussian is also a favorable case: scoring errors with heavier tails, such as a learned judge that occasionally misjudges badly, push the selected error up faster than \(\sqrt{\ln \CandidatePoolSize}\), so the drift in a real pool can exceed this estimate.

Rising selected scoring error does not by itself imply declining true utility. In Exercise 8(b), independent Gaussian utility and noise make both the winner’s expected utility and its scoring error increase with pool size. Empirical evaluations of best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling against learned reward models show a different pattern: the optimized proxy score keeps increasing while an independent reference score initially improves, plateaus, and eventually degrades (Gao et al. 2023). That downturn depends on the joint distribution of utility and measurement error, or on checks that search can exploit; equation 4 alone does not predict it. Figure 4 sketches the empirical pattern, with a rising proxy score (dashed blue curve) and a held-out reference score (solid green curve) that peaks before declining.

Verifier blind spots compound across large candidate pools following the same binomial dynamics as the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) metric. If an unmodeled edge case or exploit is present in a fraction \(\ExploitCandidateFraction\) of proposals, a batch of \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidates contains at least one exploiting solution with probability \(1-(1-\ExploitCandidateFraction)^\CandidatePoolSize\). A vulnerability that appears in only 0.5 percent of baseline candidates, practically invisible during single-shot testing, emerges in a batch of 64 candidates with probability 27.4 percent. When an invalid candidate achieves a passing score by slipping through such a blind spot, competitive selection readily identifies and crowns it. Verifier accuracy measured on unselected baseline distributions systematically overstates verification reliability during search, and this divergence widens with candidate breadth.

In the connection-pool scenario, consider an agent attempting to fix a broken notification path. The test suite pass rate can increase because a patch fixes the missing wake-up signal, or because the patch deletes the unit test assertion that detects blocked worker threads. Both alterations improve the raw score, but only the first resolves the underlying defect. The candidate under evaluation must therefore remain strictly isolated from the verification criteria, preventing generated code from tampering with authoritative test definitions or synthesizing fabricated pass signals. When candidate code executes within the same process address space as the evaluation harness, it can monkey-patch runtime assertion routines, the most acute form of adversarial Goodhart. Verification cycles generate value only while the resulting evaluation evidence remains trustworthy and uncorrupted.

↪ Grader isolation, which keeps test definitions, grading scripts, and pass signals outside the candidate’s reach, is developed in Grader isolation.

Sandboxing the evaluator preserves verification integrity but does not expand its intrinsic coverage: a sealed regression suite still misses unexercised thread schedules, and a learned judge still risks favoring persuasive but erroneous code. Verification protocols must therefore measure the end-to-end outputs produced by the deployed search policy across target token budgets, accounting explicitly for false acceptances and abstentions. Increasing confidence requires orthogonal, independent verification mechanisms; evaluating another correlated heuristic score adds negligible informational value.

Effective verification also yields fine-grained execution artifacts that subsequent search stages can reuse, including specific contradicted hypotheses, verified sub-invariants, and passing regression subsets. When an agent refines a candidate, localized modifications often leave earlier sub-verifications intact. Explicitly modeling these verification dependencies allows the search runtime to selectively invalidate obsolete assertions while preserving valid intermediate proofs, avoiding the computational overhead of rescoring unchanged reasoning trajectories from scratch.

3.5 Revisable Candidate Plans

Validation checks often challenge intermediate premises rather than merely rejecting a final answer. Suppose a software repair agent formulates a diagnosis, writes a patch, and outlines a test plan to resolve a connection-pool leak. When a targeted regression test contradicts the initial diagnosis, discarding the entire proposal wastes previous computation and discards valid discoveries. Conversely, retaining the unrevised plan leaves downstream logic anchored to a disproven assumption. To resolve this tension, the agent runtime must isolate the candidate artifacts that depend on the invalid premise before determining the scope of subsequent model invocations.

A revisable candidate plan makes these causal dependencies explicit. Rather than recording past execution history, it tracks unexecuted candidate operations alongside the specific evidence supporting each step. When new evidence undermines a premise, the runtime invalidates and regenerates only the affected subgraphs while preserving supported artifacts. The newly generated components must then satisfy the same verification checks as the original plan. This architecture descends directly from truth maintenance systems, which maintain explicit justifications for each inference so that retracting a premise automatically invalidates every conclusion resting upon it (Doyle 1979).

3.5.1 Candidate dependency graphs

Consider an agent candidate that attributes a connection-pool exhaustion failure to an overly short client timeout. The runtime organizes the candidate’s internal structure as a directed acyclic graph, placing directed edges from each prerequisite to the artifact that relies on it. Table 5 illustrates this organization. In this graph, the repository source excerpt, the initial failure log, and the test inventory are externally supplied inputs, whereas the diagnosis, the patch candidate, and the selected verification checks are generated artifacts. The graph formalizes causal relationships among these components without requiring the underlying model’s autoregressive reasoning to mirror the same explicit structure.

Now suppose a targeted concurrency test reveals that an idle connection became available while a waiting worker remained blocked past its notification threshold. This observation directly refutes the timeout hypothesis: the root cause is a missed wake-up notification rather than an aggressive timer. Because both the timeout patch and its associated check selection depend directly on the refuted diagnosis, the runtime marks them as invalid and schedules them for reconsideration. At the same time, the source excerpt remains valid because the underlying repository file has not changed, and the API compatibility analysis remains sound because public interface constraints have not shifted. The runtime constructs the next inference prompt by supplying the still-valid inputs alongside the new test observation, requesting an updated diagnosis, patch, and test suite. The new candidate artifacts are then subjected to independent verification.

Maintaining rigorous dependency tracking is essential. An incomplete graph with missing edges retains stale conclusions just as easily as an unmanaged prompt buffer. For example, if an apparently decoupled compatibility branch relies on method signatures modified by the new patch, omitting that dependency edge leaves invalidated compatibility claims in place. Explicit dependency management proves valuable precisely when the runtime overhead of maintaining the graph and traversing invalidation paths is smaller than the latency and token cost of recomputing unaffected candidate branches.

3.5.2 Evidence preconditions

The runtime must distinguish an actively contradicted claim from an observation that has simply expired or become irrelevant. A failing unit test directly refutes the hypothesis that a proposed patch resolves the defect. In contrast, a previously successful test run on an earlier candidate remains a valid historical measurement of that specific candidate, but provides zero evidence for the current variant. Disentangling historical observations from active plan validation prevents cached test successes from erroneously endorsing unverified code changes.
