To guarantee sound reuse, every observation recorded during a repair task must capture its complete provenance: the exact source revision or patch digest, test suite version, compiler flags, and runtime environment configuration. Similarly, an observation derived from reading a file must bind to the cryptographic hash of the file contents at the moment of inspection, rather than to a mutable filesystem path that could be modified concurrently.

When an input artifact changes or an assumption fails, the runtime marks that node as unsupported and traverses the dependency edges to identify all downstream artifacts requiring re-evaluation. If an artifact node \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode\) loses its supporting evidence, the set of invalidated artifacts is precisely its reachable descendants in the graph: \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode) = \{\ReachableArtifactNode \mid \InvalidatedArtifactNode \rightsquigarrow \ReachableArtifactNode\}\), where \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode \rightsquigarrow \ReachableArtifactNode\) indicates that a directed path leads from \(\InvalidatedArtifactNode\) to \(\ReachableArtifactNode\). A breadth-first search computes \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode)\) in time linear in the number of vertices and edges.5 All nodes outside \(\text{Desc}(\InvalidatedArtifactNode)\) retain their validity, preserving independent subgraphs such as the API compatibility analysis. A candidate graph of dozens or hundreds of nodes, like the schema in table 5, is traversed in a tiny fraction of the illustrative 21.07 ms it takes to generate a single token. The engineering burden therefore lies not in graph search but in capturing dependency edges accurately. In the repair, the waiter-and-return test contradicts the timeout diagnosis, traversal marks its only descendants, the patch and its check selection, for review, and the next request regenerates the diagnosis and that branch from the retained inputs (figure 5).

5 Dependency-Driven Rebuilding: Build tools solved the same problem for files. Make rebuilds a target only when one of its declared prerequisites is newer than the target (Feldman 1979), and its characteristic failure is the one described here: an undeclared dependency leaves a stale target that looks up to date. The saving is only as sound as the recorded edges.

Feldman, Stuart I. 1979. “Make — a Program for Maintaining Computer Programs.” Software: Practice and Experience 9 (4): 255–65. https://doi.org/10.1002/spe.4380090402.

Explicit version identifiers and environment hashes make artifact invalidation deterministic, but an observation is only as reliable as its boundary assumptions. For instance, an integration test that queries an external network service may fail to reproduce even if local repository files remain bit-identical. Conversely, an edit to an unrelated documentation file should not invalidate unit test results whose code paths and inputs were unaffected. Safe artifact reuse depends on verifying the full operational context (sandbox isolation, mock definitions, and declared inputs) rather than relying solely on timestamps or file paths.

Systems architects must also maintain a strict distinction between planning representations and physical execution state. The candidate graph tracks uncommitted hypotheses and their supporting rationales; in contrast, reading source files, modifying a filesystem, or running integration binaries in a sandbox produce concrete external side effects. Removing an invalidated candidate node from memory does not undo a filesystem modification or release an allocated cloud container. The agent runtime must manage physical idempotency, rollback, and cleanup through dedicated failure recovery protocols.

↪ Compensation and cleanup of external effects that outlive an abandoned candidate branch are developed in Branch-scoped compensation.

3.5.3 Revision scope

When initiating a scoped revision, the runtime constructs a targeted prompt containing the current task constraints, the still-valid upstream frontier, the empirical observations that invalidated prior hypotheses, and the precise set of nodes marked for replacement. In the connection-pool scenario, the model is prompted for an alternative failure diagnosis, a corresponding patch, and an updated verification suite, omitting unchanged API definitions and repository metadata. Once the model returns candidates, the runtime verifies both that the replacements align with the retained artifacts and that their newly declared prerequisites are satisfied. A replacement that appears locally valid may still introduce latent incompatibilities with preserved downstream nodes.

Revision mechanisms operate across three distinct tiers based on the extent of invalidation:

  1. A local repair modifies a single node while preserving its outward interface and behavioral contracts, ensuring its dependents remain valid. A typical example is replacing a malformed shell command with an equivalent invocation that produces the expected exit code, leaving the overall dependency topology unaltered.
  2. A subgraph resynthesis invalidates a target node and all its descendants. The runtime supplies the model with the intact boundary frontier and requests a generative bridge from those validated inputs to the desired terminal objectives. In our connection-pool example, this tier discards the diagnosis, patch, and test plan while preserving the source context and API compatibility constraints.
  3. Stopping deliberation applies when new evidence undermines a foundational premise of the entire task, such as a required system dependency missing from the host environment or container credentials that lack necessary write permissions. No topological revision of the plan can recover validity, so the runtime ends deliberation and escalates the failure or reports the task as unresolvable under its operating contract rather than spending further inference tokens.

The decision flowchart in figure 6 triages these three responses by screening candidate artifacts against two concrete operational checks: whether downstream dependents still have their preconditions met, and whether the task’s underlying premises remain intact.

While local repair minimizes token generation, it is not universally the optimal strategy. If an invalidation strikes at the foundational framing of a problem, or if the candidate dependency graph has grown complex and unreliable, performing full regeneration from scratch often yields a more coherent artifact that is easier to validate. However, full regeneration forces the model to reproduce already-settled material, risking accidental regressions in code sections that were previously correct. Deciding between localized revision and complete regeneration requires weighing the scope of invalidated dependencies against the combined latency, token budget, and validation cost of resynthesizing the candidate.

Napkin Math 3.5: Output cost of a local plan revision
Problem: Suppose a candidate contains 12 nodes, each requiring 150 generated tokens. New evidence leaves 3 nodes needing replacement. How much output does scoped regeneration avoid if either approach produces a candidate adequate for the same subsequent checks?

Math: Multiplying the complete node count by the output per node gives 1,800 tokens for full regeneration. Multiplying the replacement count by the same output allowance gives 450 tokens for scoped regeneration. The scoped request generates 25 percent of the full request’s output, avoiding 75 percent of that output under the equal-node-size assumption.

Systems insight: Scoped regeneration saves exactly the fraction of the plan that keeps its support. The saving shrinks linearly as the contradicted premise reaches more descendants and vanishes when every node depends on it, so once most of the plan shares the rejected assumption, full regeneration costs little more and is easier to check. The count covers generated plan tokens only; input to interpret retained constraints, integration, and checking are charged to both options.

This idealized model isolates the immediate generation savings of localized regeneration. In production systems, however, those savings must be weighed against the overhead of tracking dynamic dependencies, assembling specialized prompts with retained context, and executing verification tests on hybrid plans. When a candidate plan transitions from deliberative search to actual execution, it stops being an uncommitted dependency graph and becomes active runtime state, which the agent harness manages and durable execution persists across infrastructure faults.

↪ Persisting committed plan state across worker and infrastructure faults is developed in Durable Execution.

3.5.4 Limits on repeated revision

Iterative plan revision risks degenerating into unproductive replanning cycles. Without explicit termination policies, a model can oscillate between conflicting diagnoses, generate cosmetically distinct patches that suffer from identical logical flaws, or churn compute by reacting to non-fatal compiler warnings. The runtime must determine whether a newly proposed revision reflects genuine evidence updates or merely stochastic variation. A diagnostic warning warrants plan revision only when it directly challenges a core premise of the candidate; its severity tag alone should not trigger regeneration.

To prevent thrashing, the runtime enforces strict budget ceilings across model invocations, cumulative token counts, execution checks, and elapsed wall-clock time. Every attempted revision, including a failed local repair, consumes a portion of this allowance, and the same ceilings later bound the search as a whole (section 3.6.6). Once the budget is exhausted, the deliberative loop terminates, either reporting the task as unresolvable or triggering an authorized human-in-the-loop escalation while leaving the unverified candidate unmerged. Furthermore, detecting identical or isomorphic proposals under unchanged environmental observations serves as an immediate trigger to halt search, whereas revisiting a previously explored proposal after new test evidence has emerged constitutes a valid re-evaluation.

Explicit dependency graphs let the runtime retain proven artifacts and spend compute only where a hypothesis failed. Revision, however, improves one candidate at a time. When several partial candidates compete for the same budget, the runtime must also decide which to extend and which to abandon, account for work it has already admitted, and know when to stop extending any of them.

3.6 Bounded Search

The repair agent now holds several partial candidates at once, some freshly revised and some untouched. At each decision point it can generate an entirely new patch, extend an existing partial candidate by one step, or execute a diagnostic probe to disambiguate competing failure explanations. Each option draws against the same finite task budget while imposing distinct scheduling constraints and dependencies. A diagnostic check that gates the next code extension sits squarely on the critical path. In contrast, candidate generation requests can execute concurrently across available worker pools, whereas abandoning an unviable branch still incurs the unrecoverable cost of the tokens and runtime work already spent. Designing an effective search policy therefore requires explicit mechanisms to track admitted work, quantify pending resource consumption, and enforce principled stopping criteria.

3.6.1 Search structures

Flat sampling generates a fixed pool of complete candidates before running selection logic across them. In best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\), the runtime admits at most \(\CandidatePoolSize\) independent generation requests and applies a selector once all candidates finish. Because the requests share no execution dependencies, available serving capacity translates directly into reduced turnaround time through concurrent batching, provided the serving cluster can absorb the concurrent load without degrading token throughput. However, flat sampling defers all semantic evaluation until generation finishes. A candidate with a fatal flaw in its first five tokens still runs to completion, expending accelerator memory bandwidth and decode cycles that early validation could have saved.

Beam search6 exposes that intermediate decision boundary directly. The runtime maintains a frontier of partial candidates bounded by a fixed beam width \(\BeamWidth\). At each step, it generates a bounded set of extensions for each active candidate, evaluates the extended prefixes using a scoring function, and retains only the top-\(\BeamWidth\) candidates for the next round. If fewer candidates survive pruning than the beam capacity allows, the runtime advances only the viable survivors; the search root itself typically begins below full beam width. Step scores can combine learned model probabilities with deterministic checks available at that execution boundary. The beam width bounds the active frontier that receives subsequent generation work rather than total ephemeral state: during expansion, the system must temporarily hold \(\BeamWidth\) times the branching factor in candidate extensions, along with their KV cache state, scores, and parent references.

6 Beam Search: The method first appeared in the HARPY speech recognition system, which advanced only a small set of the best-scoring partial paths at each step instead of the full search space (Lowerre 1976). It accepted a bounded risk of pruning the eventual best path in exchange for bounded time and memory, the same trade a runtime makes when it retains a fixed number of partial candidates.

Lowerre, Bruce T. 1976. “The HARPY Speech Recognition System.” PhD thesis, Carnegie Mellon University; Carnegie Mellon University.

Early pruning trades generation compute for evaluation overhead and introduces a strict dependence on intermediate scoring accuracy. If a scoring pass mistakenly discards a prefix that would have yielded an optimal solution, a bounded beam search cannot recover that path unless the search policy explicitly incorporates backtracking. Conversely, terminating an unviable patch early avoids the cost of full candidate synthesis and subsequent integration tests. Early pruning yields net savings whenever intermediate evaluations cost less than the downstream generation they preempt, while maintaining an acceptably low false-rejection rate on viable prefixes.

An adaptive tree policy removes the constraint of expanding every retained candidate uniformly. Tree search over intermediate reasoning steps (Yao et al. 2023) dynamically steers computational effort toward branches with promising score trajectories, high outcome uncertainty, or diagnostic value for unresolved choices. Monte Carlo tree search (Coulom 2006; Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006) provides a formal framework for tracking visit counts and backpropagating estimated returns across a search graph. A prominent selection policy, the PUCT variant7 used in AlphaGo (Silver et al. 2016), traverses the tree from the root by selecting at each partial state \(\PolicyObservedState\) the action \(\PolicyAction\) that maximizes

7 From UCT to PUCT: The UCT rule treated each node’s choice of extension as a multi-armed bandit, adding an exploration bonus that shrinks as an extension accumulates visits (Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006). PUCT weights that bonus by a predictor’s prior over the arms (Rosin 2011), which is what lets a language model’s own step probabilities concentrate a small budget on plausible extensions. A miscalibrated prior steers visits away from correct but unlikely steps just as effectively.

Kocsis, Levente, and Csaba Szepesv’ari. 2006. “Bandit Based Monte-Carlo Planning.” European Conference on Machine Learning (ECML), 282–93.
Rosin, Christopher D. 2011. “Multi-Armed Bandits with Episode Context.” Annals of Mathematics and Artificial Intelligence 61 (3): 203–30. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10472-011-9258-6.

\[ \TreeSearchMeanReturn(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction) + \TreeSearchExplorationWeight\,\TreeSearchPriorDistribution(\PolicyAction \mid \PolicyObservedState)\,\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{\SiblingPolicyAction} \TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\SiblingPolicyAction)}}{1 + \TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)}, \tag{5}\]

where \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)\) represents the empirical mean return observed through extension \(\PolicyAction\), \(\TreeSearchVisitCount(\PolicyObservedState,\PolicyAction)\) is its visit count, \(\TreeSearchPriorDistribution(\PolicyAction \mid \PolicyObservedState)\) denotes a prior distribution over extensions (such as the base model’s proposal probability for that reasoning step), and \(\TreeSearchExplorationWeight\) is a positive scaling factor balancing exploitation of high-value paths against exploration of plausible but under-sampled branches. The vocabulary comes from reinforcement learning. The leaf value \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\) is a score of a partial candidate, supplied by a process scorer or by a check run at that point, and the “return” that \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn\) averages is the leaf value reported by each visit through the edge. When the runtime evaluates a newly reached leaf node and obtains value \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\), every edge along the trajectory back to the root updates its visit count (\(\TreeSearchVisitCount \leftarrow \TreeSearchVisitCount + 1\)) and its running mean value (\(\TreeSearchMeanReturn \leftarrow \TreeSearchMeanReturn + (\TreeSearchLeafScore - \TreeSearchMeanReturn)/\TreeSearchVisitCount\)). If \(\TreeSearchLeafScore\) originates from an imperfect learned scorer, the \(\TreeSearchMeanReturn\) term in equation 5 inherits the selection drift formalized in equation 4, biasing visits toward edges whose scores are elevated by estimation noise. While tree search focuses compute where value signals appear strongest, its utility hinges on whether the resulting allocation improvements outweigh the latency and memory overhead of leaf evaluations and graph state management.

The three structures differ chiefly in where pruning happens (figure 7). Best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) defers selection until every candidate is complete, beam search prunes partial candidates at each step boundary, and tree search expands one favored leaf at a time and updates visit counts along its path. Those pruning points in turn set each structure’s concurrency, retained state, and prefix sharing (table 6).

↪ Radix prefix caching, which lets later requests for a branch reuse the KV state of its shared ancestors, is developed in Radix prefix lookup.

3.6.2 Pruned tree growth

Pruning is what keeps a search tree from exploding. A search graph that unconditionally expands every node into 4 branches across 10 successive steps yields 1,048,576 leaves, quickly exhausting memory capacity and compute budgets. Now consider a system where each node proposes \(\SearchBranchingFactor\) candidate extensions, and an intermediate validation check independently prunes each child with probability \(\ChildPruningProbability\). The expected number of surviving branches per expanded node, the effective branching factor, becomes

\[ \EffectiveBranchingFactor = \SearchBranchingFactor\,(1 - \ChildPruningProbability). \tag{6}\]

Under equation 6, the expected frontier width at depth \(\SearchDepthIndex\) is \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor^{\,\SearchDepthIndex}\). Because each of the surviving nodes at depth \(\SearchDepthIndex-1\) generates and validates \(\SearchBranchingFactor\) proposed children, the total expected count of generated nodes through depth \(\SearchDepth\) is \(\sum_{\SearchDepthIndex=1}^{\SearchDepth} \SearchBranchingFactor\,\EffectiveBranchingFactor^{\,\SearchDepthIndex-1}\). This formulation defines a classic Galton–Watson branching process.8 When \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor < 1\), which requires a pruning rate above the critical threshold \(1 - 1/\SearchBranchingFactor\), the frontier contracts geometrically with depth, causing the search tree to extinguish spontaneously. When \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor > 1\), the frontier expands exponentially, requiring hard caps on depth, token consumption, or elapsed execution time to force termination.

8 Galton–Watson Process: The model comes from a Victorian question about the extinction of family surnames, analyzed in 1875 (Watson and Galton 1875). That analysis wrongly concluded that every line dies out; the correct result is the threshold used here, under which extinction is certain when the mean number of surviving offspring is at most one (excluding the degenerate case of exactly one child every time) and avoidable when it exceeds one. For a search tree the surviving offspring are the children that pass the step check, so the rejection rate decides which side of the threshold the search lands on.

Watson, Henry William, and Francis Galton. 1875. “On the Probability of the Extinction of Families.” The Journal of the Anthropological Institute of Great Britain and Ireland 4. https://doi.org/10.2307/2841222.

To see this quantitative effect in practice, consider a concrete configuration where each node generates 5 extensions, the step validator rejects 68 percent of proposals, and the search proceeds to depth 3. The resulting effective branching factor is 1.6. For \(\SearchBranchingFactor =\) 5 the critical rejection rate is 80 percent, and because the 68 percent rejection rate sits below it, the search remains supercritical (\(\EffectiveBranchingFactor > 1\)). An unpruned tree across these three steps generates 155 nodes and terminates with 125 leaves. In contrast, the pruned search generates an expected 25.8 nodes and retains approximately 4.1 leaves, eliminating 83.4 percent of total node generations. The aggregate reduction exceeds the raw 68 percent per-node rejection rate because pruning a single parent eliminates the entire combinatorial subtree beneath it. Assuming uniform step sizes, token generation savings follow the identical proportion, though each discarded node still incurs the upfront cost of its own generation and validation pass.

Each rejected child also removes the subtree it would have grown.

Two systems caveats govern this branching model. First, intermediate checks that exhibit false rejections irrevocably prune viable solution paths; aggressive pruning is therefore advantageous only when the validator’s false-rejection rate remains tightly controlled. Second, consider a single step that needs only one surviving child to advance. Proposing children one at a time requires \(1/(1 - \ChildPruningProbability)\) attempts in expectation. Proposing a batch of \(\ChildBatchSize\) children in parallel requires \(1/(1 - \ChildPruningProbability^{\,\ChildBatchSize})\) rounds instead, with each round generating \(\ChildBatchSize\) children. Batching reduces elapsed wall-clock rounds at the expense of higher aggregate token expenditure, the same trade between concurrency and total work that notebook 3.3 priced for best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling.

3.6.3 Branch isolation

While a partial patch is an ephemeral candidate data structure, evaluating it requires concrete execution against a target environment. If one search branch mutates files in a shared workspace and a sibling branch subsequently runs against that modified working tree, the sibling’s test outcomes no longer reflect its own proposed edits. A robust runtime must therefore isolate execution environments so that every observation binds strictly to the candidate patch and workspace snapshot that produced it. Stateful or mutating branches require independent execution containers, copy-on-write filesystem forks, or deterministic rollback procedures that restore a clean baseline before subsequent branches execute.

↪ Copy-on-write task workspaces that keep each run’s file modifications private are developed in Copy-on-write provisioning.

Workspace isolation incurs substantial systems overhead even when a search branch is ultimately discarded. Concurrent sandboxes consume host memory, storage volumes, container daemon process slots, and network bandwidth, while their logged outputs accumulate in the agent’s working context. Even read-only diagnostic commands require sandboxed execution permissions and can inadvertently leak environment variables or credentials into search traces. Pruning a candidate drops it from the active search frontier, but computational backtracking cannot undo remote side effects such as outbound network requests or database writes. Dedicated sandbox containment mechanisms enforce these operational boundaries, ensuring that search policies budget external actions under the reality that tree traversal cannot reverse real-world side effects.

3.6.4 A resource ledger for the whole search

Managing search budgets requires maintaining separate ledgers for financial expenditure, physical work, wall-clock time, and live system state. Financial cost aggregates generator inferences, validation calls, and external tool invocations under a unified currency. Physical work dimensions, however, represent fundamentally non-interchangeable resource demands: generator prompt and completion tokens, scoring passes, unit test executions, and sandbox setup routines each stress different infrastructure bottlenecks. Summing hardware FLOPs with end-to-end test runtimes in seconds produces meaningless telemetry.

Elapsed wall-clock latency depends entirely on how execution scheduling maps these demands onto hardware. Sequential search phases sum latency along the critical path, parallel candidate evaluations overlap across available worker threads, and limited thread or accelerator allocations force excess requests into batched waves. Peak live state introduces yet another independent constraint. It reflects the maximum footprint held simultaneously in memory and storage (active KV caches, candidate ASTs, diagnostic logs, and running sandboxes) rather than the cumulative volume of candidates generated over the task lifetime. An optimization that reduces aggregate completion tokens often increases input token reprocessing, introduces synchronization barriers, or balloons memory occupancy. Tracking these dimensions in separate ledger accounts prevents composite cost metrics from masking critical systems bottlenecks.

Napkin Math 3.6: Flat sampling vs. bounded beam expansion
Problem: A coding agent generates candidate patches in 4 segments of 128 tokens. Compare 16 complete candidates with beam width 4 and 3 extensions per retained prefix. How do early pruning and repeated scoring change the resource ledger?

Setup: Every generator request supplies the same 4,096-token task prompt plus its retained generated prefix. The illustrative per-token generation time from Per-call work estimate is 21.075 ms. Assume 8 generation streams can maintain that rate concurrently. The scorer reads the entire generated prefix, excluding the common task prompt, and processes requests serially at a fixed 15 ms per request regardless of prefix length, so the growth with prefix length that equation 2 describes appears in the scorer’s token bill rather than in this stipulated time. Each final test takes 0.5 s, with 8 isolated runs available concurrently. Generation, scoring, and final testing do not overlap in this schedule.

Hypothetical prices per million tokens are $3 for generator input, $15 for output, and $0.50 for scorer input. Input charges include every supplied token, without a prefix-reuse discount. The timing subtotal omits prefill, queueing, request transfer, workspace preparation, and cleanup. It is not a prediction of complete service latency, and the token-charge subtotal excludes execution and infrastructure charges.

Math: Multiplying 16 candidates by 512 output tokens per candidate gives 8,192 output tokens. Multiplying the same candidate count by the common prompt length gives 65,536 input tokens. Generation occupies 2 waves of complete candidates, followed by 2 test waves. Multiplying the generation waves by candidate length and the per-token allowance, then adding the test waves times test duration, gives 22.58 s. Multiplying each token count by its corresponding price gives a token-charge subtotal of $0.3195.

The beam expands frontiers of 3, 9, 12, 12 extensions in successive stages. Adding these counts gives 36 newly generated segments; multiplying by the segment length gives 4,608 output tokens. After each stage it retains at most the beam width. For each request, generator input is the common prompt plus the preceding generated segments; summing across requests gives 156,288 input tokens. The scorer instead reads each complete generated prefix, including its new segment. Summing each stage’s extension count times its prefix length gives 13,440 scorer-input tokens. Generation requires 7 waves of one-segment requests; serial scoring adds 0.54 s; the final survivors fit in 1 test wave. Applying the same duration and price arithmetic gives 19.92 s and $0.5447.

Systems insight: The beam generates 43.75 percent fewer output tokens, worth $0.054, but resupplying the task prompt and retained prefixes to every extension costs $0.272 more in input, so its token bill is the higher one. The knob is the price of repeated input: the beam’s bill falls below flat sampling’s only when input tokens cost less than 17.3 percent of list price, the regime a prefix cache creates. Its shorter generation waves already give it the lower timing subtotal under this schedule, before omitted prefill is added back. Quality is not in this ledger; the choice between the two rests on the check results each produces.

3.6.5 Admission limits and pending work

A task-wide quota must restrict new candidate admissions prior to dispatch, because capacity overruns discovered after concurrent requests complete represent non-refundable expenditures. Before dispatching any candidate extension, the runtime reserves its maximum allowed completion tokens along with all associated validation and sandbox execution allowances. The sum of settled consumption and outstanding reservations must remain strictly within configured ceilings. Unused quota reservations return to the available budget pool only after the runtime verifies that the corresponding request has concluded without further liability. Enforcing hard spending limits therefore requires strict, enforceable charge bounds before admission and authoritative settlement evidence before releasing unused reservations. A final receipt establishes what was charged but cannot bound an uncapped operation in advance; purely local accounting cannot enforce a remote billing ceiling.

↪ Bounded-liability reservation and settlement mechanisms are developed in Reservation and settlement.

Resource management policies must similarly decouple search depth from elapsed execution time. A depth ceiling bounds the chain of sequential model extensions or tool interactions, whereas a deadline bounds overall user latency. When the deadline expires, the runtime ceases admitting new work, discards late arrivals from the expired search phase, and issues cancellation requests to downstream services. However, as Streaming and cancellation establishes, transmitting a cancellation signal does not guarantee immediate remote termination; a service that does not honor it can keep decoding, and billing, up to the call’s full token allowance. Until remote serving layers settle the transaction and confirm termination, the scheduler must maintain outstanding reservations in the ledger.

Exhausting allocated resources terminates the search unconditionally without lowering acceptance thresholds. A fully generated candidate can still fail essential regression checks, and favorable model scores cannot substitute for verified test passes when authorizing actions. If no candidate satisfies the acceptance contract when budgets expire, the runtime must report an incomplete outcome, abstain, or escalate to human operators. Mechanical resource limits govern execution regardless of what the model’s output proposes, which is invariant closure (principle 1) applied to deliberation. The bound sits below the model, and acceptance still requires evidence above it.

3.6.6 Early exit and the value of continuation

Many practical workloads seek the first candidate that satisfies designated operational criteria, a strategy known as satisficing.9 Once a proposed solution fulfills required validation checks and authorization rules, the search policy terminates without exploring further alternatives. A successful compiler pass confirms only syntactic and type conformance; concluding search at that point delegates environmental testing, authorization, and final deployment to subsequent pipeline stages.

9 Satisficing: Herbert Simon borrowed the word in 1956 to describe a chooser who stops at the first alternative meeting an aspiration level, because a decision maker with limited capacity cannot afford an exhaustive search for the best (Simon 1956). A runtime’s acceptance criteria play the role of that aspiration level, which is why they must be fixed before search begins rather than lowered as the budget runs out.

Simon, Herbert A. 1956. “Rational Choice and the Structure of the Environment.” Psychological Review 63 (2): 129–38. https://doi.org/10.1037/h0042769.

In contrast, an explicit optimization objective can justify continued search even after an acceptable candidate is found. For example, a system may seek a more compact patch or one that reduces production latency, provided remaining budget permits. Conversely, an upward trend in learned heuristic scores does not warrant continuation if offline evaluations demonstrate that incremental score gains rarely improve actual acceptance rates. Stopping criteria must therefore formalize the concrete marginal value of additional computation rather than assuming that passing a preliminary check renders further search unproductive.

Marginal stopping10 explicitly evaluates whether the expected gain of the next admissible operation justifies its incremental resource cost. During an active repair session, the runtime cannot directly measure the true probability of finding a valid patch on the next attempt. Instead, it must rely on empirical success estimators calibrated across representative benchmark tasks, or on validated progress indicators such as reduced compiler warning counts or incremental test passes. Because individual diagnostics provide incomplete proxies for overall correctness, these probability estimates remain strictly conditioned on the calibrating workload distribution and validator suite.

10 Sequential Stopping: Statistics made stopping part of an experiment’s design with the sequential probability ratio test, which after each observation decides whether to accept, reject, or sample again (Wald 1945). Such tests reach a verdict with fewer observations on average than a fixed-size test with the same error rates. The marginal rule borrows that structure, although its threshold is an economic choice rather than an error rate.

Wald, Abraham. 1945. “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16 (2): 117–86. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731118.

For a nested sequence of round limits under the same workload and acceptance criterion, let \(\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount}\) estimate the probability of finding an acceptable result by round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\). Let \(\CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount}\) denote the expected cumulative expenditure of the policy that stops at its first acceptable result and otherwise runs up to that round limit. Before admitting round \(\AdmittedWorkUnitCount\), with no earlier acceptance, the marginal policy stops when

\[ \frac{\CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount} - \CalibratedAcceptanceEstimate_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}}{\CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount} - \CumulativeWorkCost_{\AdmittedWorkUnitCount-1}} < \MarginalStoppingThreshold, \tag{7}\]

where \(\MarginalStoppingThreshold\) defines the minimum acceptable rate of success probability gain per unit expenditure. The incremental expected expenditure is the cost of admitting the prospective round multiplied by the probability of reaching it without an earlier acceptance. Dividing the cumulative probability gain by that same reach probability gives the conditional chance of success in the next round. The displayed ratio therefore equals conditional next-round success gain divided by the full cost of admitting that round. A cumulative probability gain divided by the cost of a round that always runs would mix these two populations. The conversion describes the calibrated no-earlier-acceptance cohort; an adaptive runtime must condition its prospective estimates on the current evidence and search history. Completed comparable runs supply calibration data, not a retrospective instruction to admit another round.

The numerator is a gain in the estimated probability of an acceptable result, not a count of new candidates or a rise in scorer output, and the verification asymmetry (principle 3) is the reason. Producing another candidate and establishing that some candidate is valid are different events, so progress has to be measured by the second. A search that keeps generating while its calibrated acceptance estimate stays flat is spending without progress, however much output it produces. The threshold is an economic choice rather than a property of the search algorithm. The value of an additional acceptable result relative to escalation, including residual checker error and delay, sets the threshold. Severe failure costs do not by themselves justify more search against an unreliable check.

For satisficing policies, runtimes must enforce these stopping rules according to a strict three-tier precedence (figure 8). Hard resource limits on tokens, API requests, search depth, and wall-clock deadlines take absolute precedence and stop new admissions unconditionally. The runtime preserves any candidate that already meets the acceptance criteria for the acceptance decision; without one, it reports an unresolved outcome or escalates. Satisficing rules evaluate second: any candidate satisfying full acceptance criteria terminates search within the available resource envelope. The marginal stopping rule of equation 7 applies last, governing continuation only when hard limits remain unreached and no candidate has yet met acceptance criteria. Inverting this hierarchy would permit speculative value estimates to breach hard resource quotas, or allow diminishing marginal returns to abandon an already successful candidate.

Value estimates optimize how the runtime allocates resources permitted by mechanical boundaries, identifying when further execution yields diminishing returns. When a search halts, the runtime must cleanly separate verified outputs from abandoned attempts while reconciling all outstanding reservations against external services.

Checkpoint 3.2: Accounting for a bounded search

The ledger and stopping policy must account for work even when it produces no accepted result.

A search structure defines the topological paths along which an agent can allocate compute, but the ledger and stopping rules govern physical resource consumption and enforce execution safety. Determining which search policy delivers superior results requires evaluating full end-to-end performance under identical resource ceilings and environmental constraints, incorporating the true operational cost of failed and abandoned runs.

3.7 Deliberation Strategy Evaluation

Suppose an iterative search policy resolves more connection-pool failures than a single-call baseline. That improvement might reflect genuine algorithmic deliberation. It might also stem from a larger token budget, a more capable underlying model, or silently discarded hard cases. Rigorous evaluation of any policy combining candidate allocation, selection, verification checks, and early stopping must account for three things: the exact resources and permissions granted to each policy, the compute and wall-clock time actually spent, and the disposition of tasks that fail to yield an acceptable result.

3.7.1 Controlled and system-level comparisons

A controlled policy comparison holds the base model, serving configuration, benchmark workload, tool interfaces, and acceptance criteria fixed. By varying only the deliberation policy under identical resource ceilings and deadlines, it isolates how search and verification alter output quality. Engineers should specify prompt tokens, generated tokens, verification invocations, and permitted concurrency as distinct budgets rather than collapsing them into an ambiguous aggregate “compute budget.” Efficient policies often stop early; unspent budget reflects algorithmic efficiency rather than an experimental error.

A system-design comparison evaluates an end-to-end architecture across broader operational trade-offs. For example, an engineer might compare a large foundation model called once against a smaller, faster model invoked repeatedly within the same latency and dollar budgets. This benchmark characterizes the composite system, but it cannot isolate the algorithmic effect of deliberation from model capability. Maintaining a clear boundary between controlled search evaluations and macro system-design comparisons prevents teams from mistaking a raw model upgrade for an algorithmic search breakthrough.

Telemetry must track the fate of every assigned task, accounting fully for explicit failures, timeouts, and incomplete searches. To avoid crediting a search for exploiting its own scorer (the Goodhart effect of section 3.4.3), assess final output quality using an independent evaluator rather than the internal heuristic score optimized during candidate generation. Telemetry must also distinguish among three failure modes: false acceptance (committing an incorrect result), explicit abstention or refusal, and deadline exhaustion. When reporting checker errors, state the denominator explicitly:11 the fraction of incorrect candidates that pass a check differs substantially from the fraction of accepted outputs that are incorrect. Finally, because test-time search introduces stochastic branching, repeated trials must characterize distribution variance across tasks rather than presenting a single point estimate as an engineering guarantee.

11 Two Error Denominators: The fraction of incorrect candidates that pass is the check’s false positive rate, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) in this chapter; the fraction of accepted outputs that are incorrect is the false discovery rate, \(1-\VerifierPrecision\). Equation 3 connects them only through the valid-candidate rate, so a report that gives one without the base rate cannot be converted into the other.

True operational expenditure accounts for every resource consumed along the execution path: discarded branches, repeated prompt prefill across iterations, verification checker invocations, and in-flight token generation billed after a cancellation signal. Dividing total cohort cost by the number of tasks meeting the stated acceptance criterion yields the effective cost per accepted task, the cohort realization of \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2). Averaging costs over successful runs alone severely understates operational expense by ignoring the sunk cost of abandoned trajectories. Similarly, system designers should report latency distributions alongside this cost ratio, tracking the exact fraction of tasks completing before the deadline. An attractive mean latency often masks a catastrophic tail where sequential revisions render the slowest tasks unusable.

3.7.2 Policies under a deadline

To see how concurrency limits and real-time deadlines bound what a deliberation policy can achieve in production, consider an evaluation using a scoped test suite rather than an omniscient truth oracle. The probabilities below are stipulated parameters rather than empirical measurements from a specific checkpoint, isolating how concurrency, stopping rules, and execution budgets interact under a hard latency cap.

Napkin Math 3.7: Three policies, one deadline
Scenario: Consider an automated software repair workload evaluated against a fixed, protected validation suite. An initial candidate passes the suite with hypothetical probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) = 40 percent. Repeated attempts evaluate the same underlying issue, and candidate generation draws are conditionally independent given that task. Passing the suite serves as the operational acceptance criterion, though passing tests does not guarantee semantic perfection. Each policy operates under identical runtime permissions and resource bounds: ceilings of 32,768 input tokens, 8,192 generated tokens, and 8 test executions, alongside a concurrency limit of 8 parallel workers and a strict 75 s deadline.

Each initial generation request submits 2,048 prompt tokens, and every candidate generates 1,000 tokens. Executing the validation test suite requires 10 s. Candidate generation assumes an illustrative baseline service time of 21.075 ms per generated token, derived from the memory-bandwidth decode floor in Per-call work estimate. The calculation assumes that this generation rate holds across concurrent streams and that test execution runs on dedicated, non-interfering compute resources. (Prefill latency, scheduling queues, network round trips, and candidate filtering overhead are omitted here for analytical clarity.) Model serving prices are stipulated at $3 per million prompt tokens and $15 per million output tokens, without prompt caching discounts.

Option A (single candidate): Generate and validate a single candidate. The suite-pass probability is 40 percent, consuming 2,048 prompt tokens, 1,000 generated tokens, and one validation check. The end-to-end attempt finishes in 31.07 s at a model bill of $0.02114. If the candidate fails the suite, the pipeline terminates without returning a solution.

Option B (parallel breadth): Dispatch all 8 candidates concurrently without early termination, validating each as it completes. The policy selects the first passing patch according to a deterministic index ordering. The probability that at least one of the \(\CandidatePoolSize\) = 8 candidates passes the suite is pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize = 1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^{\CandidatePoolSize}\), reaching 98.32 percent. Because selection requires only a single binary pass, this also represents the probability of returning an accepted repair. Operating under full parallel concurrency, the wall-clock latency remains 31.07 s. However, this concurrent fan-out consumes 16,384 prompt tokens, 8,000 generated tokens, 8 validation executions, and incurs a model bill of $0.16915.

Option C (sequential feedback): Generate an initial candidate and terminate immediately if it passes. If the candidate fails, append the failed output and a 340-token error trace to the context before requesting a targeted revision. Conditional on an initial failure, the revised candidate has a hypothetical suite-pass probability of \(\RevisionPassProbability\) = 35 percent. Although the policy allows a nominal maximum of 3 rounds, executing three sequential attempts would demand 93.22 s, far beyond the system deadline. Within the 75 s budget, exactly 2 complete generation-and-test rounds can finish. Consequently, the deadline restricts the policy to at most one feedback revision, returning a failure if that second attempt also fails.

Math: Under deadline truncation, the cumulative pass probability for sequential feedback is \(\AttemptPassProbability+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\,\RevisionPassProbability\), or 61 percent, with an expected execution count of \(1+(1-\AttemptPassProbability)\), or 1.6 rounds. The expected resource consumption is 4,080.8 prompt tokens, 1,600 generated tokens, and 1.6 test executions, yielding an expected model bill of $0.03624. The expected completion latency is 49.72 s, bounded by a worst-case latency of 62.15 s. The remaining 39 percent of runs fail to produce an accepted candidate; these represent unrecoverable search failures under budget truncation rather than ignored experiments. Across all initiated tasks, the expected number of generated tokens per accepted completion is approximately 2,500 for Option A, 8,137 for Option B, and 2,623 for Option C.

Systems insight: Evaluated per accepted completion, the direct model cost is $0.0529 for Option A, $0.0594 for Option C, and $0.1720 for Option B, a 3.3× multiple over the single-candidate baseline. Sequential feedback raises the acceptance rate from 40 percent to 61 percent with only a modest cost premium per accepted task. Parallel breadth achieves the highest overall acceptance rate (98.32 percent), but at substantially higher cost per accepted result.

The operating deadline dictates which strategy is feasible. When the latency budget permits only a single 31.07 s round, parallel breadth is the only one of these policies that can improve on the baseline 40 percent success rate. When the deadline accommodates two rounds, sequential feedback raises the pass probability with a smaller model bill than parallel breadth. However, attempting a third round would require 93.22 s, delivering zero benefit within a 75 s service envelope. Production trade-off calculations must also incorporate test execution costs alongside token billing, while tracking false acceptance rates separately to ensure that test passage reflects authentic task resolution.

3.7.3 Operating point selection

Each policy in the comparison occupies a distinct position on the Pareto frontier. The single-candidate baseline minimizes financial and token costs, parallel breadth maximizes the probability of meeting the acceptance threshold within a tight latency budget, and sequential feedback offers an attractive compromise between pass rates and token expenditure at the expense of higher wall-clock latency. A policy configuration is strictly dominated only when an alternative feasible strategy matches or exceeds its performance on every system metric (latency, token cost, verification load, and accuracy) while being strictly superior in at least one. Non-dominated configurations define the trade-off surface from which engineers must select an operating point based on production service-level agreements, budget caps, and fault tolerance.

Deploying deliberation policies successfully requires evaluating them under the exact runtime constraints they face in production. Analytical models that assume unconstrained search cannot simply substitute an ad-hoc “effective candidate count” into an idealized binomial formula; real system concurrency bottlenecks, prefix caching efficiencies, and verification overheads distort clean theoretical scaling. Similarly, the conditional success probability of an iterative repair depends heavily on the diagnostic fidelity of the execution feedback provided to the model. Finally, hard deadlines censor execution trees, truncating planned revisions before they yield fruit. Offline benchmarking must faithfully reproduce these system limits (worker timeouts, early-exit heuristics, and resource preemption) rather than extrapolating optimistic, uncensored search curves into latency-critical production environments.

3.8 Fallacies and Pitfalls

The most consequential errors in deliberation design stem from conflating computational effort with verified task progress. In test-time search, allocating compute is an engineering investment whose return depends strictly on candidate diversity, verifier precision, and sound resource accounting. When these mechanisms are poorly calibrated, extra compute merely amplifies noise, exhausts serving budgets, or reinforces systematic model errors.

Fallacy: More generated tokens necessarily produce a better answer.

Extended deliberation can help a model unpack complex constraints already latent in the prompt, but it can just as readily elaborate and defend an erroneous initial premise. When the primary uncertainty concerns empirical behavior, such as how a program executes at runtime, generating tokens is an inefficient and indirect way to discover facts. In section 3.1, for example, generating a 1,024-token explanation takes 21.58 s, 539.5× the 40 ms execution test that supplies the missing observation directly. Raw token consumption reflects computational expenditure rather than genuine problem-solving progress. Extra reasoning tokens belong only where empirical benchmarks confirm that the resulting gain in candidate quality justifies the added latency and financial cost.

Pitfall: Treating independent samples as independent capabilities.

Candidate draws that are conditionally independent for a given prompt still share the model’s underlying parameterization and systemic blind spots. Consequently, estimating pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) from an aggregate benchmark success rate dramatically overestimates what a candidate pool can deliver (section 3.3.3). Consider a workload where half the tasks succeed at a rate of 60 percent and the other half fail consistently (\(\AttemptPassProbability = 0\)). Across the entire suite, the mean success rate is 30 percent, the same per-candidate rate that notebook 3.3 assumed for a single fixed task. Substituting that aggregate average into the independent-draw formula predicts a pool success rate of 94.2 percent for a pool of 8. In reality, the true pool success rate reaches only 50.0 percent, because repeated sampling cannot overcome failure modes that the underlying weights cannot resolve. Always compute pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) on a per-task basis first and then average the results across the workload.

Fallacy: A check with a low false acceptance rate makes almost every pass trustworthy.

Verifier precision depends just as fundamentally on the base rate of valid candidate solutions as it does on the check’s nominal error rates (equation 3). When valid solutions are rare, even a small false acceptance rate allows invalid candidates to dominate the accepted pool. For instance, consider an evaluation check with a 2 percent false acceptance rate and a 10 percent false rejection rate. When only 5 percent of generated candidates are valid, only 70.31 percent of passing candidates are genuinely correct. Under these conditions, a first-pass selector returns a defective repair on 29.26 percent of attempts (notebook 3.4). On challenging tasks, improving the generator’s baseline yield is just as decisive for precision as tightening the verification filter.

Pitfall: Charging only for the final answer.

Discarded search branches, repeated context prefill, execution diagnostics, and candidate selection all consume hardware resources, while speculative requests canceled in flight often continue to incur inference charges. Evaluating a deliberation strategy solely by the length of its final accepted response masks these true system costs. In notebook 3.7, for example, a parallel breadth search returns a single 1,000-token candidate, but it bills 8,000 output tokens per task and consumes 8,137 tokens for each suite-passing task. Similarly, the beam search in notebook 3.6 reads 156,288 input tokens simply to produce 4 survivors for final validation. Production accounting must charge the entire search ledger, including failed branches and unaccepted tasks, against the cost of each accepted solution.

Fallacy: The highest-scoring candidate has completed the task.

A scoring function ranks candidates along specific, modeled criteria, and argmax selection concentrates the evaluator’s estimation errors directly into the winning candidate. With equal true utilities and independent Gaussian scoring noise, the selected candidate’s expected score error reaches approximately 2.3 standard deviations when choosing among 64 candidates (equation 4). Furthermore, an evaluation gap or specification blind spot present in only 0.5 percent of individual candidates emerges in 27.4 percent of such pools. A top relative score provides neither authority nor evidence for requirements that the verifier does not check. If a search exhausts its budget without any candidate clearing the verification threshold, the highest-ranking candidate remains an unverified failure rather than an accepted solution.

3.9 Summary

Test-time compute transforms the cost of a decision into an explicit architectural trade-off. While model weights determine the capability of a single forward pass, the runtime system governs how much additional compute to invest, which execution topology to deploy, and what empirical evidence warrants terminating the search. Additional deliberation improves the probability of acceptance through three necessary links, each of which can fail independently: the search must elicit capability the base model lacked in a zero-shot pass, the selector must reliably identify a superior candidate from the pool, and the verification check must evaluate the exact property required for task correctness. If any link breaks, the attempt cost increases while the acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) stagnates, driving up the effective cost per accepted task \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). Systems architects must therefore judge a deliberation policy just like any other runtime component: by the accepted results it delivers per unit of compute expenditure and wall-clock latency under concrete deadline constraints.

Table 7 summarizes the quantitative relations developed throughout this chapter to price compute allocations, evaluate selectors, bound search spaces, and enforce stopping criteria.

Key Takeaways: More work needs a bounded policy
  • Observe when the question is empirical: Generating additional tokens re-evaluates information already present in the prompt, whereas executing an external check supplies new empirical evidence. In the repair workload, generating a single explanatory trace took 539.5× as long as the targeted probe that separated the two competing hypotheses.
  • A pool is only as good as its selector: The theoretical pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) rate establishes an upper bound on what a selection policy can extract, but it does not represent realized task success. Averaging pass rates across a mixed workload naively projects an overall success rate of 94.2 percent across 8 candidates, whereas the realized pool delivers only 50.0 percent; no amount of extra sampling generates a solution for tasks where the base model assigns zero probability to valid trajectories.
  • A pass inherits the base rate: When valid candidates make up only 5 percent of the initial generation pool, a verifier with a 2 percent false-acceptance rate makes only 70.31 percent of passing candidates valid. Under the fixed IID first-passing policy, selected candidates have that same precision, and adding candidates does not raise it. With equal true utilities and independent Gaussian scoring noise, among 64 candidates, the winner’s score overstates its quality by about 2.3 standard deviations of scoring error.
  • Revise along recorded dependencies: When a new environment observation invalidates an operating assumption, recompute only the downstream sub-tasks that depend on that assumption instead of regenerating the entire plan. Unrecorded execution dependencies allow stale intermediate state to propagate silently, and no subsequent revision can undo side effects already committed to external systems.
  • Budgets end searches, not uncertainty: Every discarded candidate, verifier evaluation, and failed attempt must be billed directly to the cost per accepted task \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), with hard service deadlines dictating the runtime operating point. When an execution budget or deadline expires without producing an accepted solution, the system must return an explicit unresolved outcome rather than silently defaulting to the highest-scoring unverified candidate.

The verification asymmetry (principle 3) turns into a diagnostic order for any deliberation policy whose cost per accepted task disappoints. First ask how often the pool holds a test-passing candidate, measured per task as pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\). Low measured availability makes a stronger model or escalation alternatives to further low-yield search; only a truly zero pass probability under a fixed generation policy makes additional sampling futile. Where availability is adequate, ask how far a pass can be trusted at the measured base rate, because a low \(\VerifierPrecision\) makes a better generator or a stricter check worth more than a larger pool. Only when both answers are acceptable does the question become how much search to buy, and the marginal stopping rule answers it in conditional next-round acceptance gain per admitted cost. Each step measures a different event, and at each one the runtime, not the model, decides whether to continue, abstain, or escalate.

3.10 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Allocating computational budget at decision time has deep roots in early game-playing systems. Claude Shannon’s foundational design for a chess program established the fundamental separation between generating candidate moves and scoring the resulting board positions with a heuristic evaluation function (Shannon 1950). Because game trees expand exponentially with search depth, exhaustive exploration quickly proved intractable. Heuristic search, which generates alternatives and prunes unpromising branches using domain knowledge, subsequently emerged as a central pillar of artificial intelligence (Newell and Simon 1976). Beam search entered through speech recognition (Lowerre 1976), and the two halting criteria this chapter borrows came from outside computing, Wald’s sequential analysis (Wald 1945) and Simon’s satisficing (Simon 1956), each introduced earlier in this chapter beside the mechanism it shaped.

Shannon, Claude E. 1950. “Programming a Computer for Playing Chess.” The London, Edinburgh, and Dublin Philosophical Magazine and Journal of Science 41 (314): 256–75. https://doi.org/10.1080/14786445008521796.
Fikes, Richard E., Peter E. Hart, and Nils J. Nilsson. 1972. “Learning and Executing Generalized Robot Plans.” Artificial Intelligence 3: 251–88. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(72)90051-3.
Doyle, Jon. 1979. “A Truth Maintenance System.” Artificial Intelligence 12 (3): 231–72. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(79)90008-0.
Coulom, Rémi. 2006. “Efficient Selectivity and Backup Operators in Monte-Carlo Tree Search.” Proceedings of the 5th International Conference on Computers and Games (CG 2006), 72–83. https://doi.org/10.1007/11925231_6.
Silver, David, Aja Huang, Chris J. Maddison, Arthur Guez, Laurent Sifre, George van den Driessche, Julian Schrittwieser, et al. 2016. “Mastering the Game of Go with Deep Neural Networks and Tree Search.” Nature 529 (7587): 484–89. https://doi.org/10.1038/nature16961.

Dynamic revision and dependency tracking share a parallel systems heritage. Early autonomous robotics systems such as STRIPS and PLANEX monitored plan execution against sensor inputs, triggering targeted replanning whenever an observation contradicted an assumed action precondition (Fikes et al. 1972). Truth maintenance systems (Doyle 1979) and make (Feldman 1979) then gave that idea the dependency-graph form that revisable candidate plans reuse. As search problems grew more complex, algorithms learned where to allocate deliberative compute dynamically, and Monte Carlo tree search (Coulom 2006; Kocsis and Szepesv’ari 2006) supplied the bandit-style allocation that PUCT refines. When paired with deep neural networks for move priors and position evaluation, this bandit allocation strategy proved decisive in AlphaGo (Silver et al. 2016), demonstrating how learned value functions can guide search through astronomical state spaces.

Modern language models have revived and unified these deliberate search techniques at unprecedented scale. In program synthesis, the pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) metric formalized the benefits of parallel candidate sampling evaluated against executable test suites (Kulal et al. 2019; Chen et al. 2021). For open-ended tasks lacking deterministic unit tests, Cobbe et al. (2021) demonstrated that training a dedicated verifier to rank sampled candidates could outperform parameter scaling of the generator alone. Deliberation soon expanded along complementary axes of depth and breadth. Chain-of-thought prompting elicited sequential reasoning depth by generating explicit intermediate tokens (Wei et al. 2022), while self-consistency sampling improved breadth by taking a majority vote across parallel reasoning trajectories (Wang et al. 2023). To provide feedback before a full solution finishes generating, process supervision shifted verification from final answers to individual intermediate steps (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024). This step-level verification enabled Tree of Thoughts (Yao et al. 2023) to implement systematic beam search, lookahead, and backtracking over discrete reasoning units.

Wei, Jason, Xuezhi Wang, Dale Schuurmans, Maarten Bosma, Brian Ichter, Fei Xia, Ed H. Chi, Quoc V. Le, and Denny Zhou. 2022. “Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 24824–37. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1800.
Wang, Xuezhi, Jason Wei, Dale Schuurmans, Quoc Le, Ed Chi, Sharan Narang, Aakanksha Chowdhery, and Denny Zhou. 2023. “Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models.” The Eleventh International Conference on Learning Representations.
Uesato, Jonathan, Nate Kushman, Ramana Kumar, Francis Song, Noah Siegel, Lisa Wang, Antonia Creswell, Geoffrey Irving, and Irina Higgins. 2022. “Solving Math Word Problems with Process- and Outcome-Based Feedback.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2211.14275, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2211.14275.
Yao, Shunyu, Dian Yu, Jeffrey Zhao, Izhak Shafran, Thomas L. Griffiths, Yuan Cao, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2023. “Tree of Thoughts: Deliberate Problem Solving with Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).
DeepSeek-AI, Daya Guo, Dejian Yang, et al. 2025. “DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2501.12948, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2501.12948.

Recent systems treat test-time compute as an explicit budget to optimize. Snell et al. (2024) showed that the compute-optimal balance among parallel sampling, iterative revision, and guided search is governed primarily by task difficulty. Easier problems gained most from sequential revision, moderately difficult ones from combining parallel exploration with guided search, and problems with a near-zero base success rate gained more from a larger model than from any test-time allocation. Reinforcement learning on verifiable reasoning tasks demonstrated that models can internalize this search process autonomously, learning to spend thousands of generation tokens on self-checking, exploration of alternative subproblems, and backtracking without explicit human step annotations (DeepSeek-AI et al. 2025). Pushing search hard against learned scorers resurfaces Goodhart’s law (Goodhart 1984), which Gao et al. (2023) measured as reward-model overoptimization. Under heavy best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling, a held-out reference score peaks and then declines even as the proxy score keeps rising.

  • Search as problem solving (Newell and Simon 1976). The foundational formulation of heuristic search as generating and pruning hypotheses under resource limits, establishing the architectural taxonomy that inference-time allocation policies build upon.
  • Monte Carlo tree search (Browne et al. 2012). The definitive survey of tree-search algorithms, covering the selection policies, rollout strategies, and backpropagation operators that systems engineers adapt when structuring deliberation over discrete language-model steps.
  • Trained verifiers (Cobbe et al. 2021). An early demonstration that generating multiple candidates and scoring them with a dedicated verifier outperforms scaling generator parameters alone, providing an empirical baseline for verification overhead vs. accuracy gains.
  • Process supervision (Lightman et al. 2024). A rigorous empirical comparison between outcome-based and step-level supervision, detailing the annotation costs and error-detection advantages of scoring intermediate reasoning steps.
  • Reward-model overoptimization (Gao et al. 2023). An empirical measurement of Goodhart’s law under best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling, characterizing how proxy reward models diverge from true task performance as test-time search intensity increases.
  • Compute-optimal test-time allocation (Snell et al. 2024). An empirical analysis of how the Pareto-optimal frontier between parallel sampling, iterative revision, and tree search shifts with task difficulty, offering a methodology for profiling inference-time compute budgets.
Newell, Allen, and Herbert A. Simon. 1976. “Computer Science as Empirical Inquiry: Symbols and Search.” Communications of the ACM 19 (3): 113–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/360018.360022.
Browne, Cameron B., Edward Powley, Daniel Whitehouse, Simon M. Lucas, Peter I. Cowling, Philipp Rohlfshagen, Stephen Tavener, Diego Perez, Spyridon Samothrakis, and Simon Colton. 2012. “A Survey of Monte Carlo Tree Search Methods.” IEEE Transactions on Computational Intelligence and AI in Games 4 (1): 1–43. https://doi.org/10.1109/TCIAIG.2012.2186810.
Cobbe, Karl, Vineet Kosaraju, Mohammad Bavarian, Mark Chen, Heewoo Jun, Lukasz Kaiser, Matthias Plappert, et al. 2021. “Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2110.14168.
Gao, Leo, John Schulman, and Jacob Hilton. 2023. “Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 10835–66.
Snell, Charlie, Jaehoon Lee, Kelvin Xu, and Aviral Kumar. 2024. “Scaling LLM Test-Time Compute Optimally Can Be More Effective Than Scaling Model Parameters.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2408.03314.

3.11 Exercises

Unless a problem states otherwise, use the hypothetical givens in table 8. They are problem inputs chosen for round arithmetic, not measurements of any model or service. Omit prefill, queueing, and transport time, and apply no prefix-cache discount unless a problem introduces one.

1. Three allocations, one repository (Basic). A repair request carries an \(8{,}000\)-token prompt and a ceiling of \(6{,}000\) generated tokens. Depth spends the whole ceiling on one candidate. Breadth generates six \(1{,}000\)-token candidates, but the service admits only three concurrent streams. Feedback runs \(\TurnHorizon = 3\) rounds of \(1{,}000\)-token candidates; each round ends with a 6 s check that returns a 500-token report, and later requests append every earlier candidate and report.

  1. Using equation 1, compute each allocation’s critical path, input tokens, output tokens, and model-token bill.
  2. The repository context grows to \(64{,}000\) tokens. Recompute the three bills and the breadth premium \((\CandidatePoolSize-1)\SequenceLength\) in tokens and dollars.
  3. The task owner now requires a result within 60 s. Which allocation meets the deadline, and at what bill? A prefix cache would charge re-read prompt tokens at 10 percent of list price. What does breadth then cost, and what omitted term in this accounting should an engineer measure before committing to it?

2. Sizing a pool against a target (Basic). For a fixed repair task, independent candidates pass the acceptance check with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability = 0.20\), and the selector returns a passing member whenever one exists.

  1. Find the smallest pool \(\CandidatePoolSize\) whose pass@\(\CandidatePoolSize\) reaches 90 percent, and the availability gained by its last candidate.
  2. Compare candidates spent per accepted repair for the parallel pool and for sequential retry that stops at the first pass and gives up after \(\CandidatePoolSize\) attempts. What does the parallel pool buy for its premium?
  3. The workload is actually mixed: half the tasks pass at \(0.40\) per candidate and half never pass, so the average rate is still \(0.20\). What pool success does the pool size \(\CandidatePoolSize\) from part (a) deliver, and what is the largest pool success any \(\CandidatePoolSize\) can deliver? What should the runtime do with the second half of the workload instead?
  4. In a benchmark, 4 of 20 samples for one task pass. Compute the subset estimate of pass@5 and compare it with the plug-in value \(1-(1-\PassingSampleCount/\GeneratedSampleCount)^5\).

3. How strict must the check be? (Basic). A candidate pool has valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate = 0.10\). The current check has \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.05\) and \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.01\) and costs $0.10 per run.

  1. Compute the probability of a pass \(\VerifierPassProbability\) and the precision \(\VerifierPrecision\) from equation 3.
  2. Precision must reach 95 percent. Find the largest \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) that meets the target at this \(\ValidCandidateRate\), and alternatively the smallest \(\ValidCandidateRate\) that meets it with the current check.
  3. The policy pre-generates 32 candidates and checks them in a fixed order until the first pass, abstaining if none passes. Compute the probability of any selection, the probability that the batch contains at least one false pass, and the probability of selecting a defective candidate. Explain why the second and third differ.
  4. A stricter check has \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.005\) and \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.08\) and costs $0.40 per run. A defective selection costs $2,000 in incident loss. Compare the two checks on expected incident loss plus expected checking cost per attempt, and decide which to deploy.

4. What step scoring costs (Intermediate). A learned process scorer reads a \(3{,}000\)-token task input and the growing prefix of a candidate built in \(\ScoredStepCount = 20\) steps of 150 tokens each. The runtime scores 64 candidate trajectories per task at the scorer price in table 8.

  1. Using equation 2, compute scorer input per trajectory and per task when every prefix is rescored from scratch, and when the interface supports incremental prefix reuse.
  2. For this \(\SequenceLength\) and \(\FiniteChange \ScoredPrefixLength\), beyond how many steps does the quadratic term exceed the \(\ScoredStepCount \SequenceLength\) term?
  3. Without prefix reuse, a team proposes scoring only every fourth step. Compute its scorer input and bill per task. A defective step is then caught on average 1.5 steps later, and every pruned trajectory generates those extra steps at the output price. Bound the added generation cost per task, and recommend a scoring stride with and without prefix reuse.

5. Keeping a tree finite (Intermediate). Each retained node proposes \(\SearchBranchingFactor = 4\) children of 200 generated tokens, a step check rejects each child independently with probability \(\ChildPruningProbability = 0.60\), and the search runs \(\SearchDepth = 5\) steps deep under a ceiling of \(20{,}000\) generated tokens.

  1. Compute \(\EffectiveBranchingFactor\) from equation 6 and the expected generated nodes and tokens with and without pruning. Which expected counts fall below the ceiling, and why does an expected count below it not guarantee that every run fits?
  2. What rejection rate would make the tree die out on its own, and why is a rejection rate that high rarely available?
  3. The step check also rejects valid steps with probability \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\). A correct solution needs five consecutive valid steps to survive. Compute its survival probability at \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 0.10\), and the largest \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate\) that keeps survival at or above 80 percent. What does this imply for tuning the check’s threshold?

6. A waiting customer sets the policy (Intermediate). The support agent (lighthouse 1.4) answers a policy question while the customer waits. Each request carries a \(6{,}000\)-token prompt of policy excerpts and conversation, each candidate reply is 150 output tokens, and a grounding check against the cited policy takes 1.5 s. A first reply passes with probability \(0.70\); conditional on a failed first reply, a revision that reads the check’s report passes with probability \(0.50\). Concurrent candidates are checked concurrently, and the reply must arrive within 8 s.

  1. Compute the duration of one generate-and-check round. How many feedback rounds fit the deadline, and what pass probability does feedback therefore deliver?
  2. For concurrent breadth of \(\CandidatePoolSize = 1\) to \(4\), compute pass probability, model-token bill per request, and bill per passing reply. Find the smallest \(\CandidatePoolSize\) that reaches 95 percent.
  3. The service can hold 600 concurrent decode streams for all sessions. How many simultaneous sessions does the choice in (b) support, compared with a single candidate? Which resource binds this policy?
  4. Contrast with the repair notebook (3.7), where a second feedback round fit the deadline. Name the constraint that changed the answer, and state one design change that would make feedback viable for the support agent.

7. Scoping a revision (Intermediate). A repair candidate records these generated artifacts, with their output tokens and direct prerequisites: diagnosis \(\text{Dx}\) (300; source excerpt, failure observation), pool patch \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\) (600; source excerpt, \(\text{Dx}\)), metrics patch \(\RepairPatchArtifact_2\) (200; \(\text{Dx}\)), check selection \(\text{Chk}\) (150; \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\), test inventory), API notes \(\text{API}\) (250; API declaration), release note \(\text{Rel}\) (200; \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\), \(\RepairPatchArtifact_2\), \(\text{API}\)), and migration script \(\text{Mig}\) (400; \(\text{API}\)). The labels are local names for this problem only.

  1. A waiter-and-return test contradicts \(\text{Dx}\). List \(\text{Desc}(\text{Dx})\), and compute the output tokens of scoped regeneration (the diagnosis and its descendants) against full regeneration.
  2. Instead, the API declaration changes while the diagnosis stands. Which artifacts need review, and what share of the output does scoped regeneration avoid?
  3. The pool patch in fact calls the pool’s public constructor, so \(\RepairPatchArtifact_1\) depends on the API declaration, but that edge was never recorded. After the declaration changes, which stale artifacts survive the traversal, and what could the runtime then wrongly reuse? Propose how the runtime should record edges so that this failure cannot occur silently, and state what that costs.

8. A judge under selection pressure (Advanced). The research agent (lighthouse 1.2) drafts \(\CandidatePoolSize\) candidate summaries of what it has read, and a learned judge scores each as \(\EstimatedCandidateUtility_i = \CandidateUtility_i + \CandidateScoringError_i\). True quality is \(\CandidateUtility_i \sim \GaussianDistribution(\CandidateUtilityMean, \CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation^2)\) with \(\CandidateUtilityStandardDeviation = 1.0\), scoring error is \(\CandidateScoringError_i \sim \GaussianDistribution(0, \ScoringErrorStandardDeviation^2)\) with \(\ScoringErrorStandardDeviation = 0.5\), all independent.
